ἡ γὰρ πληθώρα παρεμπτώσεώς ἐστιν αἰτία κατ’ αὐτὸν, ἐφ’ ᾗ φλεγμοναί τε συνίστανται καὶ πυρετὸς ἕπεται· πληθώρας τε γένεσιν ἐναργῶς ἐδήλωσεν ἐν τῇ προγεγραμμένῃ ῥήσει, καθ’ ἣν ἐπισάττεσθαι τὰ ἐν τῷ σώματι ἀγγεῖα καὶ ἐπιπλεῖστον 542 διατείνεσθαί φησι. τούτοις γὰρ αὐτὸς ἐχρήσατο τοῖς ῥήμασιν, ὥσπερ καὶ ὅτι διὰ τὸ μηκέτι δύνασθαι τὴν ἐπίσαξιν ἐπιδέχεσθαι, παρέμπτωσιν εἰς τὰς ἀρτηρίας γίνεσθαι τοῦ περιττοῦ, τὴν ὁρμὴν ἐκεῖσε λαβόντων τῶν πληθυνόντων κατὰ τὰς φλέβας. παραλέλειπται μὲν οὖν τι κᾀνταῦθα κατὰ τὴν διαίρεσιν. οὐ γὰρ οὕτως ἁπλῶς ἐχρῆν εἰπεῖν, ὅταν μηκέτι ἐπίσαξιν αἱ φλέβες δέχωνται, τὴν ὁρμὴν γίγνεσθαι τῶν ἐν αὐταῖς εἰς τὰ τοῦ πνεύματος ἀγγεῖα· πολλῷ δ’ ἦν βέλτιον φάναι, δυοῖν θάτερον ἕπεσθαι τῇ προειρημένῃ πληρώσει τῶν φλεβῶν, ἤτοι ῥῆξιν αὐτοῦ χιτῶνος ἢ μετάχυσιν τοῦ αἵματος εἰς τὰ τοῦ πνεύματος ἀγγεῖα. τὸ δ’ ἐξ ἀνάγκης οἴεσθαι μὲν μεταχεῖσθαι τὸ αἷμα διὰ τὸ μὴ χωρεῖσθαι πρὸς τῶν φλεβῶν; ἐπιλελησμένου παντάπασίν ἐστιν, ὡς γίγνεταί ποτε ῥῆξις ἀγγείου διὰ πλῆθος, ἵνα παραλίπω τὴν ἄνευ μεταχύσεως ἀναστόμωσιν ἐναργῶς πολλάκις εἰς αἰσθητὰς εὐρυχωρίας γιγνομένην ἐντέρων καὶ γαστρὸς καὶ στομάχου καὶ φάρυγγος καὶ στόματος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στομάχου

For plethora, according to him, is the cause of paremptosis, upon which inflammations are formed and fever follows; and the generation of plethora he showed clearly in the passage quoted above, in which he says that the vessels in the body are crammed full and distended to the greatest extent 542. For he himself used these very expressions, just as he also said that, because they are no longer able to receive the cramming, a paremptosis of the surplus into the arteries takes place, the matters abounding in the veins having taken their impulse thither. Something, then, has been left out here too in the division. For he ought not to have said thus simply that, when the veins no longer admit cramming, the impulse of their contents is toward the vessels of the pneuma; it would have been far better to say that one of two things follows upon the aforesaid filling of the veins, either a rupture of the tunic itself or a transfusion of the blood into the vessels of the pneuma. But to suppose that the blood is of necessity transfused because it is not contained by the veins is the mark of one who has altogether forgotten that rupture of a vessel sometimes occurs through plethos—to pass over the anastomosis without transfusion that manifestly often takes place into perceptible open spaces, those of the intestines and the belly and the stomachos and the pharynx and the mouth.

For according to him plethora is the cause of paremptosis, upon which inflammations arise and fever follows. He clearly set out the genesis of plethora in the passage quoted earlier, where he says that the vessels in the body are packed full and are distended 542 to the utmost. He himself used these very words, just as he also said that, because the vessels can no longer take up the packing, a paremptosis of the superfluity into the arteries takes place, the things that abound in the veins having taken their impetus there. Here too, then, something has been left out in the division. He should not have said so simply that, when the veins no longer receive the packing, the impetus of what is in them is directed into the vessels of the pneuma. It would have been much better to say that one of two things follows the aforementioned filling of the veins: either a rupture of its coat, or a transfusion of the blood into the vessels of the pneuma. To suppose that the blood is of necessity transfused because it is not contained by the veins is the mark of someone who has entirely forgotten that rupture of a vessel sometimes occurs through plethos. I pass over the anastomosis without transfusion that often plainly occurs into perceptible cavities: those of the intestines, the stomach, the gullet, the pharynx and the mouth.

Greek (Kühn)

ἀλλὰ τὸ μὲν ἐν τῇ διαιρέσει τῶν λόγων ﻿ 543 ἐλλιπὲς οὐκ ἐν τούτῳ μόνον Ἐρασιστράτῳ σύνηθες, ἀλλὰ καὶ κατ’ ἄλλα συντάγματα σχεδὸν ἅπαντα. Τὸ δ’ οὖν εἰρημένον, ὁποῖόν ποτ’ ἂν ᾖ, πάλιν ἐπισκεψώμεθα. πληρουμένων τῶν ἀγγείων μετρίως, ἑαυτῶν εὐτονώτεροι φαίνονται. πότερον ὦ Ἐρασίστρατε κᾄν μηδὲ συναύξηται τῷ πλήθει τοῦ αἵματος ἡ ῥώμη τοῦ σώματος, ἢ καὶ αὐτῆς συναυξανομένης; ἐγὼ μὲν γὰρ ἀναγκαιότατον εἶναί φημι πρὸς τὴν εὐτονίαν τῆς ἐνεργείας καὶ τὴν ῥώμην τῆς δυνάμεως αὐξάνεσθαι. καὶ οὕτως δὲ, εἰ τῇ τοῦ αἵματος αὐξήσει μόνον, τῆς δυνάμεως τὰς ἐνεργείας ἰσχυροτέρας γένεσθαι ἡγῇ, τοῦ Ἀσκληπιάδου μᾶλλον ἀνατρέπειν δόξειας τὰς δυνάμεις. οὐδὲ γὰρ ἐκεῖνος ἀπλῶς οὑτωσί φησιν, ᾧ πλέον αἷμα, πάντως τοῦτον εὐτονώτερον ὑπάρχειν, ᾧ δὲ ἔλαττον, ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἀτονώτερον, ἀλλ’ εὑρήσεις τινὰς εἰπεῖν διορισμοὺς, ὁμολογοῦντας τοῖς φαινομένοις, καίτοι οὐδεμίαν ἡγούμενον ἐν ἡμῖν εἶναι δύναμιν οὔτε ψυχικὴν οὔτε ζωτικήν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But deficiency in the division of the arguments 543 is habitual with Erasistratus not only in this case but also in nearly all his other treatises. Let us, then, examine once more what has been said, whatever it may amount to. "When the vessels are moderately filled, people appear more vigorous than they were." Is this so, Erasistratus, even if the strength of the body is not increased together with the quantity of the blood, or only when it too is increased along with it? For I, for my part, say that it is most necessary, for the vigour of the activity, that the strength of the faculty also be increased. And so, if you think that the activities of the faculty become stronger by the increase of the blood alone, you would seem to overturn the faculties even more than Asclepiades does. For not even he says simply thus, that whoever has more blood is in every case more vigorous, and whoever has less is of necessity less vigorous; rather, you will find that he states certain distinctions which agree with the phenomena, even though he holds that there is no faculty in us at all, neither psychic nor vital.

But this deficiency in the division of the arguments 543 is habitual to Erasistratus not only in this work but also in almost all his other treatises. Let us, then, examine again the statement itself, whatever it may be. "When the vessels are moderately filled, people appear in better tone than they themselves were." Is this so, Erasistratus, even if the strength of the body does not increase together with the quantity of the blood, or only when it too increases along with it? For my part, I say that for the good tone of the activity it is most necessary that the strength of the faculty also be increased. And so, if you believe that the activities of the faculty become stronger through the increase of the blood alone, you would seem to overturn the faculties more than Asclepiades does. For not even he says, in this simple way, that whoever has more blood is in every case in better tone, and whoever has less is of necessity in poorer tone; rather, you will find him stating certain qualifying distinctions that agree with the phenomena, even though he holds that there is no faculty in us, either psychic or vital.

Greek (Kühn)

ὁρῶνται γάρ τινες οἱ μὲν ἑαυτῶν ἐνίοτε πολυαιμότεροί τε ἄμα καὶ ἰσχυρότεροι 544 γεγονότες, οἱ δὲ ἰσχυρότεροί τε ἅ μα καὶ ὀλιγαιμότεροι· ὥστε ἐξανάγκης, οὔτε τῷ πλήθει τοῦ αἵματος αὐξανομένης τῆς ἰσχύος, οὔτε μειώσει καθαιρουμένης, ὀλιγωρότερον εἰρῆσθαι καὶ ταῦτα τῷ Ἐρασιστράτῳ νομιστέον. ὡς εἴ γέ τις ἀνέμνησεν αὐτὸν ὧν ἐγὼ νῦν διῆλθον, οὐκ ἂν ἠρνήσατο, τὴν τῶν ἐνεργειῶν εὐτονίαν οὐκ αὐξήσει τοῦ αἵματος, ἀλλὰ ῥώμῃ τῆς δυνάμεως ἕπεσθαι. ποιήσωμεν οὖν ἡμεῖς ἤδη τὸν λόγον αὐτοῦ τελεώτερον οἷς ἐνδεῶς εἶπε προσθέντες τὸ λεῖπον. ἔσται δὲ τοιοῦτος. αὐξανομένου τοῦ αἵματος ἅμα τῇ δυνάμει, καὶ τὴν εὐτονίαν ἀναγκαῖον αὐξάνεσθαι. καὶ μὴν εἰ καὶ τοῦτο εἴποιμεν, ἀληθὲς μέν τι λέξομεν, οὐδὲν δὲ ἡμῖν εἰς τὰ προκείμενα πλέον οὐδὲ ἐκ τοῦδε.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For some people are seen at times to have become both more full of blood than they themselves were and at the same time stronger, 544 others stronger and at the same time poorer in blood; so that necessarily, since strength is neither increased by the abundance of blood nor diminished by its reduction, one must consider that these things too were stated rather carelessly by Erasistratus. For if someone had reminded him of what I have now gone through, he would not have denied that the vigour of the activities follows not upon an increase of blood but upon the strength of the faculty. Let us then ourselves now make his statement more complete, adding what is lacking to what he said deficiently. It will be as follows: when the blood increases together with the faculty, the vigour too must necessarily increase. And yet, even if we were to say this, we shall be saying something true, but we gain nothing further for the matters at hand from this either.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀλιγωρότερον, καθαιρουμένης, οὐδὲν δὲ ἡμῖν εἰς τὰ προκείμενα πλέον

For some people are seen who have at times become both fuller of blood and stronger than they were before 544, and others who have become both stronger and less full of blood. So, since strength is necessarily neither increased by the quantity of the blood nor brought down by its diminution, we must consider that these things too were stated rather carelessly by Erasistratus. For if someone had reminded him of what I have now gone through, he would not have denied that the vigour of the functions follows not upon an increase of the blood but upon the robustness of the faculty. Let us, then, now make his argument more complete ourselves, adding what is lacking to what he stated deficiently. It will be as follows: when the blood is increased together with the faculty, the vigour too must necessarily be increased. And yet, even if we should say this, we shall be saying something true, but not even from this shall we gain anything more toward the matters before us.

Greek (Kühn)

τὸ μὲν γὰρ, οἷς ἄμφω συνηύξηται, τό θ’ αἷμα καὶ ἡ δύναμις, ηὐξῆσθαι καὶ τὴν εὐτονίαν, ἀληθῶς εἰρήσεται, τὸ δ’ οἷς ηὔξηται τὰ τῆς ἰσχύος, ηὐξῆσθαι τούτοις ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἀμφότερα ψεῦδος· οὐ γὰρ ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἡ δύναμις τῷ αἵματι αὐξηθέντι συναύξεται, ἐγχωρεῖ δέ ποτε καὶ τὴν δύναμιν μόνον· 545 ὁ γοῦν ὄντως ἀγαθὸς γυμναστὴς, εἰ παραλάβοι τινὰ πολὺ μὲν αἷμα φλεγματικὸν ὑπ’ ἀγυμνασίας ἠθροικότα, τῇ δυνάμει δὲ ἀσθενέστερον, ὀλίγον εἶναι ἀποδώσει τοῦτον ἰσχυρότερον, οὐ πολυαιμότερον, ἢ παρέλαβεν. ὥσπερ οὖν οὐκ ἀναγκαῖον, ὅσοι ταῖς ἐνεργείαις εὐτονώτεροι σφῶν αὐτῶν γεγόνασιν, ηὐξῆσθαι τούτοις τοὺς χυμοὺς, οὕτως ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστιν, ὅσοι ταῖς ἐνεργείαις σφῶν αὐτῶν εὐτονώτεροι γεγόνασιν, ἰσχυροτέραν αὐτοῖς γεγονέναι τὴν δύναμιν. οὐ μόνον δὲ ἀπὸ τῆς εὐτονίας ἀρξάμενος, ἐπὶ τὴν ῥώμην τῆς δυνάμεως ἀκολουθήσεις ἐξ ἀνάγκης, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἀπὸ τῆς δυνάμεως ἐπὶ τὴν εὐτονίαν· ὁ γὰρ ἰσχυρὸς τὴν δύναμιν εὐτονώτερος ἐν ταῖς ἐνεργείαις ἐστίν· ὥστ’ ἀλλήλοις ἀντακολουθεῖν εὐτονίαν μὲν ἐν ταῖς ἐνεργείαις ῥώμῃ δυνάμεως, ῥώμην δὲ ἐν τῇ δυνάμει τῇ τῶν ἐνεργειῶν εὐτονίᾳ· ὁπότερον γὰρ ἂν αὐτῶν ὑπάρχειν ὑπόθοιο, πάντως τούτῳ συνυπάρχει καὶ τὸ ἕτερον· οὐ μὴν ἀπό γε τοῦ πλείονος αἵματος ἡ ἀκολούθησις ἀναγκαία.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὀλίγον εἶναι

For the statement that, in those in whom both have increased together, the blood and the faculty, vigour too has increased, will be truly said; but the statement that, in those in whom strength has increased, both have of necessity increased, is false. For the faculty does not of necessity increase together with the blood when it has been increased, but it is possible that sometimes the faculty alone [increases]. 545 At any rate the truly good trainer, if he were to take over someone who through lack of exercise has accumulated much phlegmatic blood but is rather weak in faculty, will in a short time render this man stronger, not more full of blood, than he received him. Just as, then, it is not necessary that those who have become more vigorous than themselves in their activities should have had their humours increased, so it is necessary that those who have become more vigorous than themselves in their activities should have had their faculty become stronger. And not only when you begin from vigour will you of necessity follow on to the robustness of the faculty, but also from the faculty to vigour; for he who is strong in faculty is more vigorous in his activities. So that they reciprocally follow one another: vigour in the activities follows robustness of faculty, and robustness in the faculty follows vigour of the activities; for whichever of them you should suppose to be present, the other too in every case is present together with it. From the greater quantity of blood, however, the consequence is not necessary.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀλίγον εἶναι (rendered 'in a short while'; the phrase is syntactically odd and possibly corrupt, perhaps for ἐν ὀλίγῳ or ὀλίγου; it might alternatively refer to making the blood little), τὰ τῆς ἰσχύος (rendered 'what pertains to strength')

For it will be said truly that in those in whom both have increased together, the blood and the faculty, the good tone also has increased; but that in those in whom what pertains to strength has increased, both have of necessity increased, is false. For the faculty does not of necessity increase together with the blood when the blood has increased, and it is possible sometimes for the faculty alone to increase. 545 At any rate the truly good gymnastic trainer, if he should take over someone who from lack of exercise has accumulated much phlegmatic blood but is rather weak in faculty, will in a short while render him stronger, not more full of blood, than he was when he took him over. Just as, then, it is not necessary that those who have become of better tone in their activities than they themselves were should have had their humours increased, so it is necessary that those who have become of better tone in their activities than they themselves were should have had their faculty become stronger. And not only, starting from the good tone, will you of necessity proceed by consequence to the robustness of the faculty, but also from the faculty to the good tone; for the man who is strong in faculty is of better tone in his activities. So good tone in the activities and robustness of faculty follow reciprocally upon one another, good tone in the activities upon robustness of faculty, and robustness in the faculty upon good tone of the activities; for whichever of the two you suppose to be present, the other is in every case present together with it. From a greater quantity of blood, however, the consequence is not necessary.

Greek (Kühn)

οὔτε γὰρ εἰ πλέον τοῦτο τοῦ πρόσθεν, ἐξ ἀνάγκης εὐτονώτερος ὁ ἄνθρωπος, οὔτε εἰ ἔλαττον, ἀτονώτερος, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ ὅταν ἶσον, ὁμοίως 546 ἔχει δυνάμεως. ὥστ’ ἐκ τῶν εἰρημένων δῆλον, ὡς ἡ τῶν ἐνεργειῶν εὐτονία τοῦ μὲν ἐῤῥῶσθαι τὴν δύναμιν ἴδιόν τε ἅμα καὶ ἀχώριστόν ἐστι γνώρισμα, τῆς δὲ αὐξήσεως τοῦ αἵματος οὔτε ἴδιον οὔτε ἀχώριστον. ὃ δὲ ἐφεξῆς εἶπεν Ἐρασίστρατος, ἴδιον μὲν οὐκ ἔστιν, ἀχώριστον δέ ἐστι πληθώρας. ὀνομάζει δὲ αὐτὸ παρεμπίμπλασθαι, σαφέστερον δὲ ὀνομασθείη τείνεσθαι. καὶ γάρτοι καὶ τῶν γυμναστῶν οἱ ἀκριβέστεροι τὸν μέν τινα τῶν κόπων ἑλκώδη, τὸν δὲ τονώδη, τὸν δὲ φλεγμονώδη καλοῦντες, οὐδὲν ἄλλ’ ἐνδείκνυνται τοῦ παρεμπίμπλασθαι τὰ μέλη κατὰ τὸν τονώδη κόπον. τηνικαῦτα γὰρ ἑαυτῶν παρεμπεπλῆσθαι λέγουσιν ὁτιοῦν μόριον, ἐπειδὰν αἰσθάνωνταί τινος ἐν αὐτῷ τάσεως. αἰσθάνονται μὲν οὖν κᾀν τῇ φλεγμονῇ τάσεως, ἀλλ’ ἐκείνῃ μὲν ἤτοι διὰ παντὸς ἢ ὡς τὸ πολὺ σύνεστιν ὁ παρὰ φύσιν σφυγμὸς, ὅν τις ἐναργέστερον ἑρμηνεύων ἐρεῖ πόνον σφυγμώδη· οἷς δ’ οὐδὲν ἄλλο πλὴν τοῦ τείνεσθαι συμβέβηκε, τούτοις οὔτε φλεγμαίνει τὸ τεινόμενον μέρος οὔτε ἐξ ἀνάγκης πληθωρικὸν ὑπάρχει, ἀλλὰ δυοῖν θάτερον, ἤτοι κοπῶδες ἢ πληθωρικόν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: παρεμπίμπλασθαι, ἑλκώδη

For neither, if this is greater than before, is the man of necessity more vigorous, nor, if it is less, less vigorous, just as neither, when it is equal, is he in a similar condition with respect to power. 546 So that it is clear from what has been said that the vigour of the activities is a sign at once proper and inseparable of the power's being strong, but of the increase of the blood it is neither proper nor inseparable. But what Erasistratus said next is not a proper sign of plethora, though it is an inseparable one. He names it 'being filled up' (παρεμπίμπλασθαι), but it would more clearly be named 'being stretched' (τείνεσθαι). And indeed the more precise of the gymnastic trainers too, when they call one of the fatigues ulcerous, another tensive, and another inflammatory, indicate nothing other than that the limbs are filled up in the case of the tensive fatigue. For they say that any part whatsoever of themselves has been filled up at the moment when they perceive some tension in it. Now they perceive tension also in inflammation, but with that there is present, either always or for the most part, the pulsation contrary to nature, which someone expressing it more vividly will call throbbing pain; but in those to whom nothing else has happened besides being stretched, in these the part that is stretched is neither inflamed nor is it of necessity plethoric, but one of two things, either fatigued or plethoric.

For neither, if this is more than before, is the person of necessity more vigorous, nor, if less, more lacking in vigour, just as neither, when it is equal, is he in the same 546 condition as regards capacity. So from what has been said it is clear that the vigour of the activities is a sign at once peculiar to and inseparable from the capacity's being strong, but of the increase of the blood it is neither peculiar nor inseparable. What Erasistratus said next, however, is not peculiar to plethora, but is inseparable from it. He names it 'being filled up' (parempímplasthai), but it would be named more clearly 'being stretched'. And indeed the more precise among the trainers, calling one of the fatigues ulcerative, another tensive, and another inflammatory, indicate nothing other than the limbs' being filled up in the tensive fatigue. For they say that any part whatever of themselves has been filled up at the time when they perceive some tension in it. Now they perceive tension in inflammation as well; but with that there is present, either always or for the most part, the pulsation contrary to nature, which someone expressing it more vividly will call a pulsating pain. In those, however, to whom nothing else has happened except being stretched, the part that is stretched is neither inflamed nor of necessity plethoric, but one of two things: either fatigued or plethoric.

ἀλλ’ εἴπερ 547 κοπῶδές ἐστιν, ἐπὶ πόνοις πλείοσιν οὕτως ἔπαθεν· εἰ δ’ ὅλως οὐκ ἐπόνησεν ὁ ἄνθρωπος, οὐ διὰ κόπον κοπῶδες αὐτὸ γίγνεται τὸ μόριον, ἀλλ’ ἑτέρως· ὥστ’ ἀληθὲς εἰπεῖν, ἐπεὶ τάσεως αἴσθησίς ἐστιν ἄνευ τοῦ γυμνάσασθαι, πάντως εἶναι πληθώραν. γίγνεται δὲ, οὕτως εἰπόντων, ἀχώριστόν τε ἅμα καὶ ἴδιον τὸ σημεῖον, ἀκολουθούντων ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἀλλήλοις ἑκατέρων· ἥ τε γὰρ ἄνευ τῶν γυμνασίων τάσις ἕπεται πληθώρᾳ· γίγνεται γὰρ αὐτῆς ἀχώριστον σημεῖον, ὡς ὑπὸ αἰτίου τῆς πληθώρας ἀποτελούμενον· ἡ δὲ ἐπὶ τοσοῦτον αὔξησις τοῦ αἵματος, ὡς μηδέπω διατείνειν τὰς φλέβας, οὐδέπω πληθώρα. τί ποτ’ οὖν ἐμνημόνευσεν ὁ Ἐρασίστρατος ἐν τῷ λόγῳ βραχιόνων καὶ κνημῶν καὶ χειρῶν; καὶ γὰρ καὶ ἐν τραχήλῳ καὶ μεταφρένῳ καὶ ὀσφύϊ καὶ κατὰ θώρακα καὶ καθ’ ὑπογάστριον ἡ τοιαύτη συνίσταται τάσις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But if 547 it is fatigue-like, it was affected in this way following rather many exertions; and if the person did not exert himself at all, the part does not become fatigue-like because of fatigue, but in some other way. So that it is true to say that, since there is a sensation of tension without one's having exercised, there is in every case plethora. And, when we put it in this way, the sign becomes at once both inseparable and proper, since each of the two necessarily follows upon the other: for tension without exercises follows upon plethora, since it becomes an inseparable sign of it, as being brought about by plethora as its cause; whereas an increase of the blood to such a degree as not yet to distend the veins is not yet plethora. Why ever, then, did Erasistratus make mention in his account of upper arms and shanks and hands? For such tension arises also in the neck and the back and the loins and in the thorax and in the hypogastrium.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὕτως εἰπόντων, ὡς ὑπὸ αἰτίου τῆς πληθώρας ἀποτελούμενον, μεταφρένῳ

But if 547 it is fatigued, it has been so affected after rather many exertions; whereas if the person has not exerted himself at all, the part itself becomes fatigued not through fatigue but in some other way. So it is true to say that, since there is a perception of tension without one's having exercised, there is in every case plethora. And when it is stated in this way, the sign becomes at the same time both inseparable and proper, the two following each other of necessity. For tension without exercise follows upon plethora, since it becomes an inseparable sign of it, as being brought about by plethora as its cause; while an increase of the blood to such a degree as not yet to distend the veins is not yet plethora. Why, then, did Erasistratus in his account make mention of arms and shins and hands? For such tension also arises in the neck, the upper back, the loins, the chest and the hypogastrium.

Greek (Kühn)

ὥσπερ δὲ τῶν κώλων ὁ Ἐρασίστρατος ἐμνημόνευσεν ἐκ περιττοῦ, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον καὶ μειζόνως καὶ τῆς ἑλκώδους αἰσθήσεως· οὐ γὰρ πληθώρας αὕτη γνώρισμά ἐστιν, ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ δακνώδεσι γίνεται χυμοῖς, οἵαπερ τοῖς ἡλκωμένοις μέρεσιν, ἤτοι ψαύοντος αὐτῶν τινος, ἢ ἰχῶρος ἐρεθίζοντος, ﻿ 548 ἢ φαρμάκου δάκνοντος, ἢ κᾀν ταῖς κινήσεσι μόναις γίγνεται. ὥσπερ γὰρ ἡ τάσις οἰκεία ἐν τῷ πλήθει χυμῶν ἐστι διατεινόντων τὰ περιέχοντα, οὕτως ἡ ἑλκώδης αἴσθησις ἕπεται δακνώδει ποιότητι· καὶ διὰ τοῦτο συνίσταται πολλοῖς ἐνίοτε καὶ καθ’ ὅλον τὸ σῶμα, μὴ ὅτι πληθώραν ἔχουσιν, ἀλλὰ μηδὲ τὸ σύμμετρον αἷμα. Καὶ εἴπερ ταῦθ’ οὕτως ἔχει, καθάπερ οὖν ἔχει, καιρὸς ἤδη διορίσασθαι περί τινος ὧν ἔμπροσθεν ἀνεβαλλόμεθα, ἐπειδὴ ἐσκοπούμεθα μέχρι τοῦ δεῦρο τὰ ὑπ’ Ἐρασιστράτου γεγραμμένα σημεῖα. τὸ γάρτοι πλῆθος, εἴτ’ οὖν πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν, εἴτε πρὸς τὴν τῶν περιεχόντων χώραν νοοῖτο, βέλτιον μὲν ἦν δήπου διορίσασθαι, πότερον ἐν αἵματι μόνῳ, ἢ καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις ἅπασι χυμοῖς συνίστασθαι πέφυκε· παραλέλειπται δὲ καὶ τοῦτ’ αὐτοῖς ἀδιόριστον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οἵαπερ

But just as Erasistratus mentioned the limbs superfluously, in the same way, and even more so, he also mentioned the ulcerous sensation; for this is not a sign of plethora, but arises upon biting humours, of the kind that arises in ulcerated parts, either when something touches them, or when an ichor irritates them, 548 or when a drug bites them, or even arises in movements alone. For just as tension is proper to a plethos of humours distending the containing vessels, so the ulcerous sensation follows upon a biting quality; and for this reason it is sometimes present in many people even throughout the whole body, not merely when they have no plethora, but when they do not even have the commensurate amount of blood. And if these things are so, as indeed they are, it is now time to make a determination concerning one of the matters which we deferred earlier, since up to this point we were examining the signs written down by Erasistratus. For as regards plethos, whether it be conceived in relation to the faculty or in relation to the space of the containing vessels, it would surely have been better to determine whether it is of a nature to arise in blood alone, or also in all the other humours; but this too has been left undetermined by them.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οἵαπερ, κᾀν, δύναμιν, σύμμετρον

Just as Erasistratus mentioned the limbs superfluously, in the same way, and still more so, did he mention the ulcerous sensation. For this is not a mark of plethora; it arises with biting humours, such as occurs in ulcerated parts, whether something touches them, or ichor irritates them, 548 or a biting drug, or it arises even in movements alone. For just as tension is proper to an abundance of humours that stretch what contains them, so the ulcerous sensation follows a biting quality. For this reason it sometimes arises in many people even over the whole body, people who not only do not have plethora but do not even have the due measure of blood. And if these things are so, as indeed they are, it is now time to determine something about one of the matters we postponed earlier, since up to this point we were examining the signs written down by Erasistratus. For the plethos, whether it be conceived in relation to the capacity or in relation to the space of what contains it, it would surely have been better to determine whether it naturally arises in blood alone or also in all the other humours. But this too has been left undetermined by them.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπεὶ τοίνυν οὐ μόνον ταῦτα τὰ παθήματα καταλαμβάνει τοὺς χυμοὺς, ἔνδειά τε καὶ πλῆθος, ἀλλὰ καὶ ποιότης ἄτοπος, πάντας ἠρώτησα τοὺς ἰατροὺς, ὅσοι τε πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν νοοῦσι τὸ πλῆθος ὅσοι τε πρὸς τὴν αὔξησιν τῶν ὑγρῶν, πότερον 549 αὐξηθέντος μόνου τοῦ αἵματος τοσοῦτον, ὡς μὴ στέγεσθαι, τείνεσθαι συμβήσεται ταῖς φλεψί· εἰ δ’ ἤτοι φλέγμα εἰς ὁτιοῦν ἄλλο μόριον, ἢ καὶ αὐτὰς τὰς φλέβας, ἢ καὶ σὺν τούτῳ τε καὶ χωρὶς τούτου χολά τις, ἢ ἰχὼρ, ἢ πνεῦμα φυσῶδες ἐς πλῆθος ἀθροισθείη τοσοῦτον, ὡς μὴ χωρεῖσθαι μηκέτι πρὸς τοῦ περιέχοντος, οὐχὶ καὶ νῦν ἀναγκαῖον ἔσται, τάσεως αἰσθέσθαι τὸ ζῶον; ἂν ὑπάρχῃ τὸ τεινόμενον μόριον αἰσθητικὸν, ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστιν ἐπὶ παντὶ τῷ διατείνοντι καὶ μὴ στεγομένῳ τὴν αἴσθησιν τῆς τάσεως τῷ ζώῳ γενέσθαι, καὶ τὸ πάθημα αὐτὸ καταλαμβάνειν τὰ μόρια, τὴν ὀνομαζομένην τάσιν, ἧς ἐπὶ πλέον αὐξανομένης, ἀνάγκη τε καὶ ῥαγῆναι τὸ τεινόμενον. ἐμοὶ μὲν τοιοῦτον δοκεῖ τοῖς ἐναργῶς φαινομένοις ὁμολογεῖν. ὁρῶνται γὰρ οὐ νεῦρα μόνον, ἢ σχοινία ῥηγνύμενα τῷ βιαίῳ τῆς τάσεως, ἀλλὰ καὶ πίθοι πολλάκις ὑπὸ γλεύκους πνευματωθέντος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πνευματωθέντος

Since, then, not only these affections, deficiency and plethora, befall the humours, but also an abnormal quality, I asked all the physicians, both those who understand plethora relative to the faculty and those who understand it relative to the increase of the fluids, whether, 549 when the blood alone has been increased to such a degree as not to be contained, it will come about that the veins are stretched; but if either phlegm, into any other part whatsoever, or into the veins themselves, or, together with this or apart from this, some bile, or ichor, or flatulent pneuma should be gathered into so great a quantity as to be no longer held by what contains it, will it not in this case too be necessary that the animal perceive tension? If the part that is being stretched is capable of sensation, it is necessary, in the case of everything that distends and is not contained, that the perception of tension arise in the animal, and that the affection itself, the so-called tension, befall the parts; and when this increases further, the thing being stretched must also burst. To me, at any rate, such a view seems to agree with what is evidently observed. For not only sinews or ropes are seen bursting from the violence of the tension, but often also wine-jars, from must that has turned to pneuma.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φλέγμα εἰς ὁτιοῦν ἄλλο μόριον, ἢ καὶ αὐτὰς τὰς φλέβας, πνευματωθέντος, νεῦρα

Since, then, the humours are seized not only by these affections, deficiency and plenitude, but also by an abnormal quality, I asked all the physicians, both those who conceive of plenitude in relation to the capacity and those who conceive of it in relation to the increase of the fluids, whether, 549 if the blood alone has increased so much that it is not contained, it will happen that the veins are stretched; but if either phlegm into any other part whatever, or into the veins themselves, or, either together with this or apart from it, some bile, or ichor, or flatulent pneuma should be collected into a quantity so great that it can no longer be held by what surrounds it, will it not be necessary in this case too that the animal perceive tension? If the part being stretched is capable of sensation, it is necessary that, in the case of everything that distends and is not contained, the perception of tension arises for the animal, and that the affection itself seizes the parts, the one called tension; and when this increases further, it is necessary that the part being stretched also ruptures. To me, at any rate, such a view seems to agree with what is evidently apparent. For not only sinews or ropes are seen bursting from the violence of the tension, but often jars as well, through must that has become filled with pneuma.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κώλῳ

Recently, at any rate, a tin vessel was so greatly inflated by the drug contained in it, the one prepared from vipers, that both the leather wrapped around it and the cord with which it had been tied burst, and 550 the lid was bulged outward. And indeed that some people are at times seized by the greatest pains when flatulent pneuma has been trapped in certain parts of the animal, and that this is wont to happen especially in the colon, I think is known to all who take care to follow and examine what is seen every day in the case of the sick. It is better, therefore, to hold that the parts are brought into a tensive condition by every moist and pneumatic substance, than that such an affection follows upon blood alone among the humours when it increases in quantity. Let us pass again, then, to those who think that plethora is always constituted in relation to the faculty, and let us ask these too one of the most obvious questions, positing some such hypothesis as the following, by way of example.

Recently, at any rate, a tin vessel was so inflated by the drug contained in it, the one made from vipers, that both the skin placed around it and the cord with which it had been tied down burst, and 550 the lid was bulged outwards. Moreover, I think that all who take care to follow and examine what is seen day by day in patients know that some people are sometimes seized by very great pains when flatulent pneuma is trapped in certain parts of the animal, and that this is accustomed to happen most of all in the colon. It is better, then, to hold that the parts are brought into a tensive condition by every moist and pneumatic substance than that such an affection follows upon blood alone among the humours when it is in excess. Greek (Kühn)

(μόρια) βασταζέτωσαν ἄνθρωποι δύο κατὰ τῶν ὤμων ἀναθέμενοι ὁ μὲν ξύλον, ἢ λίθον, ἤ τι φορτίον βαρύτατον, ὁ δὲ ἕτερος ἀκάνθας ὀλιγίστας, ὀξυτάτας· εἶτ’ ἐρωτήσωμεν ἐπὶ τῷ τοιούτῳ θεάματι τοὺς πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν ἀεὶ νοοῦντας τὸ πλῆθος, εἰ καὶ τὰς ὀλίγας ἀκάνθας ὀνομάζουσι φορτίον, ἢ τὸ μὲν ἀνιᾶσθαι πρὸς ἑκατέρου τῶν βασταζομένων 551 κοινόν τι σύμπτωμα τοῖς ἀνθρώποις ἐστὶν, ἡ δὲ τῆς ἀνίας ἰδέα πάμπολυ διαλλάττουσα· τῷ μὲν γὰρ βαρύνεσθαι χωρὶς τοῦ νύττεσθαι, τῷ δὲ νύττεσθαι χωρὶς τοῦ βαρύνεσθαι συμβέβηκεν. ὅλως δ’ ἄν τις τὸ νύττεσθαι, ἢ δάκνεσθαι, ἢ ὁπωσοῦν ἀνιῶν φορτίον καλῇ καὶ πλῆθος, ἀναγκασθήσεταί ποτε καὶ μίαν ἄκανθαν ἢ βελόνην λεπτοτάτην καλεῖν φορτίον, οὗ τί ἂν εἴη γελοιότερον; ἔστιν οὖν ἄτοπα ταῦτα καὶ παρὰ τὰς κοινὰς ἁπάντων ἀνθρώπων ἐννοίας. ἡμῖν δὲ οὐκ ἀνατρέπειν, ἀλλὰ φυλάττειν τὰς κοινὰς ἐννοίας πρόκειται. πολὺ μὲν οὖν ἡγούμεθα τὸ βαρῦνον, ἀνιαρὸν δὲ τὸ δάκνον, ἢ νύττον, ἢ ὅπως ἄν τις ὀνομάζειν ἐθέλῃ. καὶ μὴν ὅτι δακνώδεις πολλοὶ τῶν χυμῶν εἰσιν, οἱ μὲν ἐν ἡμῖν αὐτοῖς τὴν γένεσιν ἔχοντες, οἱ δὲ ἔξωθεν ἡμῖν ἐπιφερόμενοι, παντὶ τοῦτο πρόδηλον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: (μόρια)

(parts) Let two men carry burdens, having set them upon their shoulders, the one a piece of wood, or a stone, or some very heavy load, the other a very few, very sharp thorns; then, in the face of such a spectacle, let us ask those who always conceive of plethora in relation to the faculty whether they also call the few thorns a load, or whether being distressed by each of the things carried 551 is a symptom common to the two men, while the form of the distress differs very greatly: for to the one it has happened to be weighed down without being pricked, to the other to be pricked without being weighed down. And in general, if someone calls being pricked, or bitten, or being distressed in any way whatsoever a load and a plethora, he will at some point be compelled to call even a single thorn or a very fine needle a load—than which what could be more ridiculous? These things, then, are absurd and contrary to the common notions of all men. But it is our purpose not to overturn but to preserve the common notions. We hold, then, that what weighs down is 'much', whereas what bites, or pricks, or however one may wish to name it, is 'distressing'. And indeed that many of the humors are biting, some having their generation in ourselves, others being brought to us from outside, this is evident to everyone.

(parts) Let two men carry, having placed it upon their shoulders, the one a log, or a stone, or some very heavy load, the other a very few, very sharp thorns. Then, at such a spectacle, let us ask those who always conceive of plethora in relation to the strength whether they also call the few thorns a load, or whether being distressed by each of the things carried 551 is a symptom common to the men, while the form of the distress differs very greatly: for it has befallen the one to be weighed down without being pricked, the other to be pricked without being weighed down. And in general, if someone calls being pricked, or being bitten, or what causes distress in any way whatever a load and a plethora, he will at some point be compelled to call even a single thorn or a very fine needle a load; and what could be more ridiculous than this? These things, then, are absurd and contrary to the common notions of all men. Our purpose, however, is not to overturn but to preserve the common notions. We therefore regard what weighs down as 'much', and what bites, or pricks, or however one may wish to name it, as distressing. And indeed that many of the humours are biting, some having their origin within ourselves, others being brought upon us from outside, this is evident to everyone.

Greek (Kühn)

εἰσὶ δ’ αὐτῶν τέτταρες ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον διαφοραί· μετρίως μὲν γὰρ δάκνοντες κνησμώδεις εἰσὶν, ὥσπερ ἡ σκίλλα· σφοδρότερον δὲ, δακνώδεις, ὥσπερ τὸ κρόμμυον· εἰ γὰρ ἐπὶ πλέον ὁμιλήσειεν ἡμῶν τὸ σῶμα τοιαύτῃ φύσει σώματος, ἑλκωθήσεται· 552 τὰ δὲ ἔτι μᾶλλόν ἐστι τούτου δριμέα, καὶ μάλιστα ὅταν ἡλκωμένοις προσφέρηται, φρίκην ἐργάζεται. φρίκης δὲ ἐκταθείσης τε καὶ αὐξηθείσης τὸ σύμπτωμα ῥῖγος ὀνομάζεται. κατὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν ἀναλογίαν ἐν ἡμῖν συνίστανται δακνώδεις χυμοὶ κατὰ τέτταρας ἰδέας· κνησμώδεις μὲν γὰρ ἐξ αὐτῶν εἰσιν οἱ μετρίως ἀμύττοντες, ἑλκώδη δὲ αἴσθησιν ἐμποιοῦσιν οἱ τούτων δακνωδέστεροι, φρίκην δὲ οἱ τούτων μᾶλλον δάκνοντες, οἱ δ’ ἱκανῶς δακνώδεις τὸ ῥῖγος. λέλεκται δὲ περὶ τούτων ἐν πολλαῖς πραγματείαις, ἀλλ’ ἱκανὰ τῷ βουληθέντι σύνοψίν τινα λαβεῖν αὐτῶν τὰ ἐν τῷ δευτέρῳ τῶν ἐν τοῖς συμπτώμασιν αἰτιῶν εἰρημένα καὶ τῷ τετάρτῳ τῶν ὑγιεινῶν· εἰ δὲ καὶ τὸ περὶ τῆς τῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως ἀναλέξαιτό τις γράμμα καὶ τὴν προηγουμένην αὐτῶν πραγματείαν τὴν περὶ κράσεων, ἐπιστημονικώτερον ἂν οὕτως πεισθείη περὶ τῆς τῶν δακνωδῶν φύσεως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

There are four differences among them in respect of the more and the less: for those that bite moderately are itch-producing, like squill; those that bite more vehemently are biting, like the onion—for if our body should be in contact for a longer time with such a nature of body, it will be ulcerated; 552 and those that are still more pungent than this, and especially when they are applied to ulcerated parts, produce shivering. And when the shivering has been extended and increased, the symptom is named rigor. By the same analogy there arise in us biting humours according to four forms: for of these, those that scratch moderately are itch-producing; those more biting than these produce an ulcerous sensation; those biting more than these, shivering; and those sufficiently biting, rigor. These matters have been discussed in many treatises, but for one who wishes to obtain some synopsis of them, what has been said in the second book of On the Causes in the Symptoms and in the fourth of the Hygiene is sufficient; and if one were also to read through the work On the Power of Drugs and the treatise that precedes these, the one On Mixtures, he would in this way be persuaded more scientifically about the nature of biting things.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀμύττοντες, ἐν τοῖς συμπτώμασιν αἰτιῶν, φρίκην

There are four differences among them according to the more and the less. Those that bite moderately are itch-producing, as squill is; those that bite more vehemently are mordant, as the onion is; for if our body were to be in contact for longer with a body of such a nature, it will be ulcerated. 552 Things still more acrid than this, especially when they are applied to ulcerated parts, produce shivering; and when shivering is prolonged and intensified, the symptom is called rigor. By the same analogy, mordant humours form in us in four kinds. Those of them that scratch moderately are itch-producing; those more mordant than these produce an ulcerous sensation; those that bite more than these, shivering; and those that are exceedingly mordant, rigor. These matters have been discussed in many treatises, but for anyone who wishes to get a synopsis of them, what is said in the second book of the Causes in Symptoms and in the fourth of the Hygiene is sufficient. If someone were also to read through the treatise on the power of drugs, and the treatise that precedes it, the one on mixtures, he would in this way be persuaded in a more scientific manner about the nature of mordant things.

Greek (Kühn)

ἀλλὰ οἱ πολλοὶ τῶν ἰατρῶν ὅμοιόν τι πεπόνθασι τῷ πρὸς Ἱπποκράτους εἰρημένῳ περὶ τῶν ἐπὶ κατάγμασιν ἢ τοιούτοις τισὶ παθήμασι τὰ προσταττόμενα μὴ ποιούντων· καὶ γὰρ κᾀκεῖνοι, καίτοι ὑγιεῖς ἐθέλοντες γενέσθαι, ﻿ 553 τὸν ἐν τῇ θεραπείᾳ κάματον οὐχ ὑπομένουσιν, ὥσπερ οὗτοι τὸν ἐν τῷ μανθάνειν. ἀλλ’ οὔτε ἐξαίφνης ὑγιεῖς οἷόν τε γενέσθαι μόνῳ τῷ βούλεσθαι τυχόντας τοῦ τέλους, οὔτε τούτους ἐπιστημονικοὺς, ἄνευ τοῦ διελθεῖν ἅπασαν τὴν ὁδὸν, ἣν ἀρτίως ἐδήλωσα. γνώσονται γὰρ, εἰ βουληθεῖεν, ὡς ἡ ἑλκώδης ὅλου τοῦ σώματος αἴσθησις, ἥ τε ἐπὶ πλέοσι γυμνασίοις ἀήθως παραληφθεῖσι γιγνομένη καὶ ἡ χωρὶς γυμνασίων ἄφνω συστᾶσα, δακνώδους ἔγγονος ὑπάρχει χυμοῦ. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τοὺς πλέον γυμνασαμένους ἐλαίῳ τε πλείονι καὶ τρίψει μαλακῇ καὶ λουτροῖς γλυκέων ὑδάτων θερμοῖς ἀκριβῶς εὐκράτοις οἱ γυμνασταὶ θεραπεύουσιν, ὡς διαφορηθῆναι τοὺς δακνώδεις χυμοὺς, ὅσοι γυμνασαμένων ἀμετρότερον ἐγεννήθησαν ἐπὶ συντήξει πιμελῆς καὶ σαρκὸς μαλακῆς, ἢ δι’ αὐτοὺς τοὺς ἐν τῷ σώματι χυμοὺς ἰσχυρῶς θερμανθέντας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But the majority of physicians have suffered something similar to what was said by Hippocrates concerning those who, in the case of fractures or some such affections, do not do what is prescribed; for those too, although they wish to become healthy, 553 do not endure the toil involved in the treatment, just as these do not endure the toil involved in learning. But it is not possible either for the former to become healthy all at once, attaining the end by the mere wishing, or for the latter to become scientific without traversing the whole road which I have just now set out. For they will come to know, if they should wish it, that the ulcerous sensation of the whole body, both that which arises upon more numerous exercises undertaken contrary to habit and that which forms suddenly without exercises, is the offspring of a biting humour. And for this reason the gymnastic trainers treat those who have exercised too much with more abundant oil and soft rubbing and warm baths of sweet waters that are precisely well-tempered, so that the biting humours may be dispersed—all those that were generated, when they exercised rather immoderately, upon the colliquation of fat and of soft flesh, or on account of the humours in the body themselves having been strongly heated.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συντήξει, ἀήθως παραληφθεῖσι, δι’ αὐτοὺς τοὺς ἐν τῷ σώματι χυμοὺς

But most physicians have suffered something similar to what was said by Hippocrates about those who, in fractures or some such affections, do not do what is prescribed. For they too, although wishing to become healthy, 553 do not endure the toil involved in the treatment, just as these do not endure the toil involved in learning. But it is neither possible to become healthy all at once, attaining the end by merely wishing it, nor for these men to become scientifically knowledgeable without traversing the whole road which I have just now indicated. For they will recognise, if they are willing, that the ulcerous sensation of the whole body, both that which arises upon rather many exercises undertaken in an unaccustomed manner and that which comes on suddenly without exercises, is the offspring of a biting humour. And for this reason the trainers treat those who have exercised too much with more oil, soft rubbing, and hot baths of sweet waters, exactly well-tempered, so that the biting humours may be dispersed: those which, in people who have exercised rather immoderately, were generated upon a colliquation of fat and soft flesh, or on account of the humours in the body themselves having been strongly heated.

Greek (Kühn)

εἴ τις οὖν ὑπομείνειε τὰς τούτων ἀποδείξεις ἐκμανθάνειν, ἐκεῖνος ἂν μόνος πεισθείη βεβαίως, τὴν μὲν ἑλκώδη διάθεσιν ἐπὶ δακνώδεσι γίγνεσθαι χυμοῖς, τὴν δὲ τοῦ βάρους ἐπὶ πολλοῖς πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν, τὴν δὲ τῆς τάσεως ἐπὶ πολλοῖς μὲν καὶ ταύτην, ἀλλ’ 554 ὡς πρὸς τὴν περιέχουσαν ἑαυτοὺς χώραν, οὐ τὴν δύναμιν τῶν (περιεχομένων) σωμάτων. Ἐρασίστρατος δὲ ἐν τῷ προτέρῳ τῶν ὑγιεινῶν, αὐτὴν μὲν τὴν γένεσιν τῆς πληθώρας διερχόμενος, ἐναργέστατα διδάσκει τὴν τρίτην τῶν εἰρημένων διάθεσιν, ἑξῆς δὲ ἐπὶ τὰ γνωρίσματα τρεπόμενος, οὐ μᾶλλόν τι ταύτης ἢ τὰ τῶν ἄλλων ἔγραψεν. ἡ μὲν γὰρ τάσις ταύτης μόνης, ὅπερ ἐκεῖνος ὠνόμασε παρεμπίμπλασθαι· τὸ δὲ ἑλκώδη γενέσθαι τὴν αἴσθησιν οὔτε τοῦ προειρημένου πλήθους οὔτε τοῦ πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν ἴδιον γνώρισμα· πολὺ δὲ μᾶλλον οὐδὲ τὸ βαρυτέρους τε καὶ δυσκινητοτέρους ἀποτελεῖσθαι· καὶ γὰρ καὶ τοῦτο ἴδιόν ἐστι τοῦ πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν πλήθους.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: περιεχομένων, παρεμπίμπλασθαι

If, then, someone should endure to learn thoroughly the demonstrations of these matters, he alone would be firmly persuaded that the ulcerous condition arises in consequence of biting humours, the condition of heaviness in consequence of humours that are many in relation to the faculty, and the condition of tension in consequence of humours that are many, this one too, but 554 in relation to the space that contains them, not to the faculty of the (contained) bodies. Erasistratus, in the first book of his Hygieina, when going through the very genesis of plethora, teaches most clearly the third of the conditions mentioned; but when he next turns to the signs, he has written no more the signs of this condition than those of the others. For tension belongs to this one alone—what he named paremimplasthai (being filled up alongside); but the sensation's becoming ulcerous is the proper sign neither of the aforesaid plethos nor of the plethos in relation to the faculty; and still less is their being rendered heavier and harder to move; for this too is proper to the plethos in relation to the faculty.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: (περιεχομένων) — bracketed in Kühn; the sense would expect περιεχόντων ('containing'); rendered as printed, 'contained', παρεμπίμπλασθαι — Erasistratus' term, rendered 'being over-filled', πολὺ δὲ μᾶλλον οὐδὲ — elliptical; taken as 'much less still is ... such a sign'

If, then, anyone should persevere in learning the demonstrations of these things thoroughly, he alone would be firmly persuaded that the ulcerous condition arises from biting humours; that of heaviness from humours that are many in relation to the capacity; and that of tension from humours that are, this too, many, but 554 in relation to the space that contains them, not to the capacity of the (contained) bodies. Erasistratus, in the first book of his *Hygieina*, while going through the very genesis of plethora, teaches the third of the conditions mentioned most clearly; but turning next to the signs, he wrote those of this one no more than those of the others. For tension belongs to this one alone, which is what he called 'being over-filled'. But the sensation becoming ulcerous is a proper sign neither of the plenitude just mentioned nor of that in relation to the capacity; and much less still is becoming heavier and more sluggish in movement such a sign, for this too is proper to the plenitude in relation to the capacity.

ὀλίγου τοίνυν ἐδέησεν αὐτῷ πᾶσαν ἡμῖν γράψαι τὴν τῶν ἐμπειρικῶν συνδρομήν· ἀμέλει καὶ μέρος τι τῆς τριχρόνου λεγομένης σημειώσεως, ἣν οἱ περὶ τὸν Ἡρόφιλον εἰσήγαγον ὁμοίως τοῖς ἐμπειρικοῖς, ἔγραψεν ἐν τῷ οὕτω φάναι· τῆς γὰρ ἀναδιδομένης τροφῆς μήτε καταπεττομένης μήτε ἐκπονουμένης κατὰ τὸ εἰθισμένον ἑκάστῳ, μήτε ἄλλως πως ἐκκρινομένης οὐδὲν γὰρ διήνεγκεν ἢ ταῦτα εἰπεῖν, ἢ ὡς ἐκεῖνοι λέγουσιν, 555 ἀργὸν βίον, ἐπίσχεσίν τε συνήθων ἐκκρίσεων. ἀργὸν μὲν γὰρ τὸν ἔμπροσθεν βίον ἐνεδείξατο, μήτε ἐκπονουμένης, φήσας, κατὰ τὸ εἰθισμένον ἑκάστῳ· τὴν δὲ τῶν ἐκκρίσεων ἐπίσχεσιν ἐν τῷ προσγράψαι, μήτε ἄλλως ἐκκρινομένης, (ἡ διὰ χρόνον σημείωσις τοῦ πλήθους) ἐκ μὲν τοῦ προγεγονότος χρόνου λαμβάνων τὸν ἀργὸν βίον ἅμα τῇ τῶν ἐκκρίσεων ἐπισχέσει· ἐκ δὲ τοῦ νῦν ἐνεστῶτος τό τε παρεμπίμπλασθαι καὶ τὴν ἑλκώδη διάθεσιν, ὄκνον τε πρὸς τὰς κινήσεις, καὶ βάρος ὅλου τοῦ σώματος, ὅσα τε ἄλλα τοιαῦτα προστιθέασιν· ἐκ δὲ τοῦ μέλλοντος, ὅτι περ κενωθέντες οἱ οὕτως ἔχοντες καλῶς ὤνηνται, ὅπερ Ἐρασιστράτῳ παραλέλειπται. μαρτύριον μὲν γὰρ τοῦτο τῆς ὀρθῆς διαγνώσεώς τε ἅμα καὶ θεραπείας ἐστί· διαγνώσεως δὲ, ἢ ὡς ἐκεῖνοι καλοῦσι, σημειώσεως, οὐδὲν ἂν εἴη μέρος, εἴ γε διὰ τῆς σημειώσεως σημειοῦσθαι βουλόμεθα τὰς διαθέσεις, ἵνα ἐξεύρωμεν τὴν προσήκουσαν θεραπείαν. ὥστε εἴ γε ἐξ ἄλλου τινὸς οἷόν τ’ ἦν ὁρμηθέντας θεραπεῦσαι προσηκόντως, ἡ σημείωσις ἂν ἦν περιττή.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἡ διὰ χρόνον σημείωσις τοῦ πλήθους

So he very nearly wrote out for us the whole syndrome of the Empiricists; indeed, he even wrote a part of the so-called three-time semeiosis, which the followers of Herophilus introduced in the same way as the Empiricists, when he spoke thus: 'for when the nutriment that is being distributed is neither fully concocted nor worked off by exertion according to what is customary for each person, nor in some other way excreted' — for it makes no difference whether one says these things or, as they put it, 555 'an idle life and retention of the habitual excretions.' For he indicated the preceding idle life by saying 'nor worked off by exertion according to what is customary for each,' and the retention of the excretions by adding 'nor otherwise excreted,' (the semeiosis of plethos by means of time) taking from the time that has gone before the idle life together with the retention of the excretions; from the now present time the being filled up and the ulcerous condition, reluctance toward movements, and heaviness of the whole body, and whatever other such things they add; and from the future, that those in such a state, once evacuated, have been well benefited — which has been left out by Erasistratus. For this is a testimony to the correct diagnosis and at the same time to the treatment; but it would be no part of diagnosis — or, as they call it, semeiosis — if indeed it is through semeiosis that we wish to infer the conditions from signs, in order that we may discover the appropriate treatment. So that if it were possible, setting out from something else, to treat appropriately, the semeiosis would be superfluous.

He very nearly, then, wrote out for us the whole syndrome of the Empirics. Indeed, he also wrote a certain part of the so-called three-time semeiosis, which Herophilus and his followers introduced, as the Empirics also did, when he spoke thus: 'For when the nourishment that is distributed is neither concocted nor worked off by exertion in the way customary for each person, nor excreted in any other way'. It made no difference whether one says this or, as they say, 555 'an idle life and retention of the customary excretions'. For he indicated the idle previous life by saying 'nor worked off by exertion in the way customary for each person', and the retention of the excretions by adding 'nor excreted in any other way' (the semeiosis of the plenitude through time). From the time that has gone before he takes the idle life together with the retention of the excretions. From the time now present he takes the being overfilled and the ulcerous condition, reluctance towards movements, heaviness of the whole body, and whatever other such things they add. From the future he takes the fact that those in this state are much benefited when they have been evacuated, and this Erasistratus has omitted. For this is a testimony to correct diagnosis and therapy together. But it would be no part of diagnosis, or, as they call it, semeiosis, if indeed we wish through semeiosis to signify the conditions so that we may find the appropriate therapy. So if it were possible to treat appropriately by starting from something else, semeiosis would be superfluous.

Greek (Kühn)

τοῦ δ’ ἐνεστῶτος χρόνου μεμνημένος, ἔρευθός τε καὶ διάτασιν ἀγγείων 556 παραλέλοιπεν, ἃ καὶ αὐτὰ προστιθέασιν οἱ ἐμπειρικοὶ σχεδὸν ἅπαντες, οὐκ ὀλίγοι δὲ τῶν δογματικῶν, ὥσπερ καὶ ἄλλα τινὰ, καθάπερ εἴρηται. καίτοι γε ὧν ὡς ἐν αἵματι μόνῳ τὴν τῆς πληθώρας ὑποτίθεται γένεσιν, οὐ κακῶ, ἐπ’ ἐκείνων πρόσκειται τῇ διαγνώσει τὸ ἔρευθος ἥ τε διάτασις τῶν ἀγγείων· ἔτι γε μὴν μᾶλλον, ὡς τὸ παρεμπίμπλασθαι φάναι βραχίονάς τε καὶ χεῖρας καὶ κνήμας, οὐ μᾶλλον τοῦ κατὰ τὰς σάρκας τε καὶ ὅλους τοὺς μύας πλήθους, ἢ τοῦ τῶν φλεβῶν θήσεται, ἰδίαις ὑποθέσεσιν ἑπόμενος. ἀλλά τοι καὶ οὕτως ἀναγκαῖον ἦν διττὴν ποιήσασθαι διάγνωσιν, ἑτέραν μὲν τοῦ τῶν αἰσθητῶν τούτων φλεβῶν, ἃς ἅπαντες ἄνθρωποι βλέπομεν· ἑ τέραν δὲ, ἣν ἐκεῖνος ἐθεάσατο μόνος. οἶμαι τοίνυν ἤδη πρόδηλον γεγονέναι τοῖς γε προσέχουσι τὸν νοῦν, ὡς οὐ μικρὸν εἴη πρᾶγμα διαγνῶναι πλῆθος. ὅπου δ’ ἂν οὐδ’ ἄχρι λόγου διαγνῶναι ῥᾴδιον, ἀλλ’ ἄπορον παντοίως ἐστὶ, σχολῇ γ’ ἂν ἐπὶ τῶν ἔργων ἀπὸ ταὐτομάτου κατορθωθείη μήτε προδιασκεψαμένοις τι περὶ αὐτοῦ μήτ’ ἐξευροῦσιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὧν ὡς, κακῶ, ὡς τὸ παρεμπίμπλασθαι φάναι, θήσεται

And in making mention of the present time, he has omitted redness and distension of the vessels, 556 which almost all the empiricists, and not a few of the dogmatists, also add, just as they add certain other things, as has been said. And yet, for those who posit that the genesis of plethora occurs in blood alone, it is not wrongly that redness and the distension of the vessels are added to the diagnosis in their case; and still more so, since he will set down the statement that the upper arms and hands and lower legs are filled up as a sign no more of the plethos in the flesh and the whole muscles than of that of the veins, following his own hypotheses. But even so it was in any case necessary to make a twofold diagnosis, one of the plethos of these perceptible veins, which all of us human beings see, and the other of that which he alone beheld. I think, then, that it has by now become plain, to those at least who pay attention, that it is no small matter to diagnose plethos. And where it is not easy to diagnose it even at the level of argument, but it is in every way perplexing, scarcely indeed could it be set right in actual practice by mere chance by those who have neither examined anything beforehand concerning it nor discovered anything.

And yet, for those who suppose that the genesis of plethora is in the blood alone, it is not badly that the redness and the distension of the vessels are added to the diagnosis in their case. 557 Let us, then, begin the discussion of plethos once more from the Empiricists, since they seem to escape quite safely from the aporiai that have been mentioned, by admitting that they themselves know nothing at all about the substance of plethos, while asserting that in the case of a certain syndrome of the following kind the removal of blood has been observed to be beneficial—a syndrome which they call plethoric. For, having gathered together into one everything that has been stated by each of the Dogmatists, they suppose that on this basis they escape the aporiai of those men, yet they are refuted far more seriously than they, if one attends carefully to what they say—all of which it is better to go through again from the beginning.

ἔστω τοίνυν ἐν μὲν τῷ προγεγονότι χρόνῳ συνήθης μὲν ἐπὶ τῶν ἐξ ἀρχῆς ἐδεσμάτων ἡ δίαιτα, καί τισιν αὐτῶν ἐν πλησμονῇ μᾶλλον ἢ πρόσθεν, ἐπίσχεσις δὲ τῆς ἐκκρίσεως, ὅ τε σύμπας βίος ἀργός· ἐν δὲ τῷ παρόντι τάσις ὅλου τοῦ σώματος, ἤ τινων μορίων, ὄκνος τε πρὸς τὰς κινήσεις, ἀτονία τε περὶ τὰς ἐνεργείας, καὶ βάρους αἴσθησις, ἑλκώδους τε διαθέσεως, ἅμα τῷ τῶν φλεβῶν ὄγκῳ· προσκείσθω δὲ, εἰ βούλει, καὶ τὸ ἔρευθος, ἐπειδὴ καὶ τοῦτο ﻿ 558 λέγουσιν ἔνιοι, καὶ μὴν καὶ ὁ τοῦ σύμπαντος σώματος ὄγκος, ἀνώμαλός τέ τις καὶ κοπώδης διάθεσις· εἰσὶ γὰρ οἳ καὶ ταῦτα προστιθέασιν. ἐρωτήσομεν οὖν αὐτοὺς, ἅπερ ἐγὼ μειράκιον ὢν ἠρώτησα τὸν ἐμπειρικὸν διδάσκαλον, ὅτε με πρῶτον ταῦτα ἐδίδασκεν, ἆρά γε πάνθ’ ἅμα τὰ εἰρημένα συνελθεῖν δεῖ πρὸς τὴν, ὡς αὐτοὶ λέγουσιν, ἀνάμνησιν τῆς κενώσεως, ἢ κᾂν ἓν ἐξ αὐτῶν θεάσωνται, φλεβοτομήσουσιν αὐτίκα; πρὸς μὲν οὖν τὴν πρώτην ἐρώτησιν ἑτοίμως ἀπεκρίνατό μοι, πάντα φάσκων συνελθεῖν χρῆναι· τοιοῦτον γὰρ πρᾶγμα τὴν συνδρομὴν ὑπάρχειν, ἄθροισμα τῶν συμπληρούντων αὐτὴν ἁπάντων.

Let it be supposed, then, that in the preceding time the regimen was the customary one with respect to the foods used from the beginning, and in some of them in greater repletion than before, with a retention of the excretion, and the whole manner of life idle; while in the present there is tension of the whole body, or of certain parts, sluggishness toward movements, weakness in the activities, and a sensation of heaviness and of an ulcerous condition, together with the swelling of the veins; and let there be added, if you wish, the redness too, since this too 558 is stated by some, and indeed also the bulk of the whole body, and a certain uneven and fatigue-like condition; for there are those who add these as well. We shall ask them, then, what I myself, when I was a youth, asked my Empiricist teacher when he first taught me these things: whether all the things mentioned must come together at once for what they themselves call the recollection of the evacuation, or whether, even if they see one of them, they will phlebotomize at once? To the first question, then, he readily answered me, saying that all must come together; for the syndrome is a thing of such a kind, a collection of all the items that make it complete.

Let it be supposed, then, that in the preceding time the regimen was the customary one, consisting of the foods taken from the beginning, and, with some of them, in greater repletion than before; that there was retention of excretion; and that the whole manner of life was idle. And let it be supposed that at present there is tension of the whole body, or of certain parts; reluctance towards movements; lack of tone in the activities; and a sensation of heaviness and of an ulcerous condition, together with distension of the veins. Let there be added, if you wish, redness as well, since some 558 mention this too; and moreover the distension of the whole body, and a certain uneven and lassitudinous condition, for there are those who add these too. We shall therefore ask them what I, as a youth, asked my Empiric teacher when he first taught me these things: must all the things mentioned concur at once for what they themselves call the 'recollection' of evacuation, or, even if they observe just one of them, will they at once perform venesection? To the first question he readily answered me, saying that all must concur, for the syndrome is just such a thing: an aggregate of all the things that make it up.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπειδὴ δὲ πρῶτον μὲν ἀνέμνησα δυοῖν ἀνθρώπων, οὓς ἔναγχος αὐτὸς ἐκεῖνος ἐκέλευσε φλεβοτομηθῆναι, τὸν μὲν ὀφθαλμιῶντα σφοδρῶς, τὸν δὲ ἐκπεσόντα τοῦ ὀχήματος, εἶτ’ ἐμπλακέντα γε τοῖς ἱμᾶσι καὶ συρέντα ἐπὶ πλέον, ὡς θλασθῆναί τινα μέρη· μετὰ δὲ ταῦτα διασιωπήσαντος αὐτοῦ, καὶ ἄλλου τινὸς ἀνεμνήσθην, ὃν καὶ αὐτὸν πυρέττοντα μὲν μετρίως, οὐ φέροντα δὲ τὸ ἄλγημα τῆς κεφαλῆς, ἐφλεβοτόμησεν· ἄλλον δὲ πλευριτικὸν, 559 οὐδὲ τοῦτον ἔχοντα τὴν εἰρημένην ἅπασαν συνδρομήν· ἐν ἀπορίᾳ κατέστη μεγίστῃ. καὶ τοίνυν καὶ διῄειν ἐπὶ πλέον αὐτῷ καὶ ἄλλους μέν τινας ὁμοίους, ἀλλὰ καὶ περὶ τῶν εἰρημένων ἀνεμίμνησκον, ὅτι τοσούτῳ δέοντας αὐτοὺς ἅπασαν ἔχειν τὴν συνδρομὴν ἐφλεβοτόμησεν, ὥστ’ ἐγὼ μὲν ἔφην οὐδὲ δύο τινὰ τούτων ἀναμιμνήσκεσθαι τοῖς ἀνθρώποις ὑπάρχοντα. τὸν μὲν γὰρ τοῦ ὀχήματος ἐκπεσόντα, γυμναζόμενον ὁσημέραι, καὶ πάσαις ταῖς συνήθεσιν αὐτάρκως ἐκκρίσεσιν ἐκκενούμενον, ἐσθίοντά τε σύμμετρα, καὶ σχεδὸν ὅλης ἡμέρας ἡμῖν συνδιατρίβοντα, καὶ μήτε ἐκείνῳ μήτε ἄλλῳ τινὶ δοκοῦντα δεῖσθαι κενώσεως, ἐπειδὴ κατενεχθεὶς ἐθλάσθη τινὰ μόρια, φλεβοτομίας ἠξίωσε, οὔτε ἔρευθός τι προσκτησάμενον, οὔτ’ ἀγγείων ὄγκον, οὔτ’ ἄλλο οὐδὲν ἐκ τῶν τῆς συνδρομῆς, ἀλλὰ μόνον ἀλγοῦντα τὰ τεθλασμένα μόρια.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἱμᾶσι

But when I first reminded him of two men whom that very man had recently ordered to be phlebotomized—the one suffering severely from ophthalmia, the other having fallen from a carriage and then become entangled in the reins and dragged for a considerable distance, so that certain parts were bruised—and when, after this, he kept silent, I recalled yet another man, whom he also phlebotomized although he had only a moderate fever but could not bear the pain of his head; and another, a pleuritic, 559 this one too not having the whole aforementioned syndrome; he was reduced to the greatest perplexity. And so I went on at greater length to recount to him certain other similar cases as well, but I also reminded him concerning those already mentioned, that he had phlebotomized them when they were so far from having the whole syndrome that I, for my part, said I could not recall even any two of these signs being present in the men. For the man who fell from the carriage—one who exercised every day, and was sufficiently evacuated by all the customary excretions, and ate in due measure, and spent nearly the whole day in our company, and seemed neither to him nor to anyone else to need evacuation—when, having been thrown down, he had certain parts bruised, he judged him in need of phlebotomy, though he had acquired neither any redness, nor swelling of the vessels, nor anything else from the signs of the syndrome, but only had pain in the bruised parts.

For the man who fell from the vehicle, who exercised daily, was adequately evacuated by all his customary excretions, ate in due measure, spent nearly the whole day in our company, and seemed neither to him nor to anyone else to be in need of evacuation, he judged to warrant phlebotomy because, having been thrown down, he was bruised in some parts, although he had acquired neither any redness, nor swelling of the vessels, nor anything else of the components of the syndrome, but only had pain in the bruised parts.

Greek (Kühn)

κατὰ ταὐτὰ δὲ καὶ τὸν ὀφθαλμιῶντα καὶ τὸν τὴν κεφαλὴν ὀδυνώμενον, οὐδὲν ὅλως ἔχοντας τῶν εἰρημένων ἐν τῇ πληθωρικῇ συνδρομῇ, καὶ ἄλλους δὲ πολλοὺς ὑφ’ ἡμῶν οἶδα φλεβοτομηθέντας, ἤτοι μηδ’ ὅλως ἔχοντας μηδὲν ἐκ τῆς πληθωρικῆς συνδρομῆς, 560 ἢ οὐ πάντα γε ἅμα τὰ πρὸς ἡμῶν λεγόμενα. νυνὶ γὰρ, ἔφην, ἀναμιμνήσκων ἐμαυτὸν, οὓς εἶδον ὑφ’ ὑμῶν φλεβοτομηθέντας, οὐδενὸς ἐξ αὐτῶν εὐπορῶ τοῦ τὴν ἐν τοιούτοις συμπτώμασιν ἅπασαν ἔχοντος συνδρομήν· ἀλλ’ ὁ μέν τις, ὅτι κοπώδης ἦν, ἐφλεβοτομήθη, μηδὲν ἄλλο ἔχων· ὁ δὲ, ὅτι πυρέττων σφοδρῶς, ἢ ἀλγῶν ὀφθαλμοὺς, ἢ κεφαλὴν, ἢ αἱμοῤῥοΐδας ἐπεσχημένος, ἢ καταμήνια γυνὴ, οὐ πάντα δὲ, ὅσαπερ ἡ πληθωρικὴ συνδρομὴ βούλεται συνελθεῖν. εἰ μὲν οὖν ἁπάσας τὰς διαθέσεις, ἐφ’ αἷς τετήρηται φλεβοτομία συμφέρουσα, πληθωρικὰς ὀνομάζεις, ὥρα λέγειν ἡμᾶς ὀφθαλμίαν, πλευρῖτιν, ἄλγημα σφοδρὸν, ἐξ ὀχήματος κατάπτωσιν, ἕτερά τε μυρία, πληθωρικὰς συνδρομάς· εἰ δὲ τούτων οὐδὲν ἐν τῇ πληθωρικῇ συνδρομῇ περιέχεται, τὸ δὲ τῶν συμπτωμάτων ἄθροισμα τῶν ἔμπροσθεν εἰρημένων ὀνομάζεται πληθωρικὴ συνδρομὴ, οὐδένα χρὴ φλεβοτομεῖν πρὶν ἅπαντα ἔχειν αὐτά.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑφ’ ἡμῶν, πρὸς ἡμῶν

In the same way too the man with ophthalmia and the man suffering pain in the head, who had none at all of the things mentioned in the plethoric syndrome; and I know of many others besides who were venesected by us, either having nothing at all from the plethoric syndrome, 560 or at any rate not all together of the things stated by us. For now, I said, as I recall to myself those whom I have seen venesected by you, I can find none among them who had the whole syndrome consisting in such symptoms; but one was venesected because he was in a state of lassitude, having nothing else; another because he was fevering violently, or suffering pain in the eyes, or the head, or had his haemorrhoids suppressed, or, in the case of a woman, her menses, but not all the things which the plethoric syndrome requires to come together. If, then, you call plethoric all the conditions in which venesection has been observed to be beneficial, it is time for us to call ophthalmia, pleurisy, violent pain, a fall from a carriage, and countless other things plethoric syndromes; but if none of these is contained in the plethoric syndrome, and it is the aggregate of the symptoms previously mentioned that is named the plethoric syndrome, then one must venesect no one before he has all of them.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑφ’ ἡμῶν (first occurrence; sense and the later ὑφ’ ὑμῶν suggest 'by you', possibly a corruption), πρὸς ἡμῶν λεγόμενα (possibly for ὑμῶν, 'stated by you'), ἐν τοιούτοις συμπτώμασιν

In the same way also the man suffering from ophthalmia and the man suffering pain in the head, who had none at all of the things stated in the plethoric syndrome; and many others too I know to have been bled by us, either having nothing whatever from the plethoric syndrome, 560 or at any rate not all together the things stated by us. "For just now," I said, "recalling to myself those whom I saw bled by you, I cannot find a single one among them who had the complete syndrome in such symptoms. Rather, one was bled because he was in a state of lassitude, having nothing else; another because he was violently feverish, or was suffering pain in the eyes, or in the head, or had had haemorrhoids suppressed, or, being a woman, her menses; but not all the things which the plethoric syndrome requires to concur. If, then, you call plethoric all the conditions in which venesection has been observed to be beneficial, it is time for us to call ophthalmia, pleurisy, violent pain, a fall from a vehicle, and countless other things plethoric syndromes. But if none of these is contained in the plethoric syndrome, and it is the aggregate of the symptoms stated earlier that is called the plethoric syndrome, then one ought to bleed no one before he has all of them."

Greek (Kühn)

πῶς μὲν οὖν ἐκεῖνος ἀπήντησέ μοι πρὸς ταῦτα, καὶ οὐδὲν πρέπον ἀπεκρίνατο, μακρᾶς ἔργον σχολῆς, 561 οὐ τῶν νῦν ἡμῖν προκειμένων ἔργον οἰκεῖον· ἐγὼ δὲ ἐπειδήπερ οὐ σχολὴν ἀδολεσχεῖν ἄγω νῦν, ἔγνων μὴ νῦν, ἐν δὲ τῷ περὶ φλεβοτομίας λόγῳ τοὺς σκοποὺς ἅπαντας ἐπιδεῖξαι προῄρημαι τοῦ βοηθήματος, οὓς οὐδ’ αὐτούς τις ἀκριβῶς τε καὶ διωρισμένως ἔγραψε. Ἐάσας οὖν ἐν τῷ παρόντι τὸ πρὸς τοὺς ἐμπειρικοὺς, αὖθις ἐπάνειμι πρὸς τοὺς λογικοὺς, ἕκαστον τῶν συμπτωμάτων ὧν ἔγραψαν ἐπισκοπούμενος. ἔνια μὲν γὰρ αὐτῶν τοῦ πρὸς τὴν δύναμίν εἰσι πλήθους, ἔνια δὲ τοῦ κατὰ τὸ ἔγχυμα, τινὰ δὲ οὐχ ἁπλῶς ἐστι σημεῖα πλήθους, ἀλλὰ τοιοῦδε πλήθους, τινὰ δὲ ὅλως οὐδενὸς πλήθους, ἀλλ’ ἤτοι κακοχυμίας τε καὶ διαφθορᾶς, ἢ ἀῤῥώστου δυνάμεως, ἢ ὄγκου τῶν στερεῶν σωμάτων. ὅτι δὲ οὕτω τοῦτ’ ἔχει, μάθοις ἂν, εἰ προσέχοις τοῖς λεχθησομένοις τὸν νοῦν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔγχυμα, ἔγνων μὴ νῦν ... προῄρημαι

How, then, that man met me in reply to these points, and gave no fitting answer, is a task for long leisure, 561 not a task proper to what now lies before us; but I, since I have at present no leisure for idle talk, have decided not now, but in the discourse on phlebotomy — there I have chosen to set forth all the indications of the remedy, which not even themselves has anyone written down precisely and with proper distinctions. Leaving aside, then, for the present the argument against the Empiricists, I return again to the Rationalists, examining each of the symptoms which they wrote down. For some of them belong to the plethora relative to the faculty, some to the plethora according to the filling (ἔγχυμα); some are not simply signs of plethora, but of a plethora of such-and-such a kind; and some are signs of no plethora at all, but either of cacochymia and corruption, or of a weak faculty, or of bulk of the solid bodies. That this is so, you would learn if you applied your mind to what is about to be said.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἔγνων μὴ νῦν, ἐν δὲ τῷ περὶ φλεβοτομίας λόγῳ ... προῄρημαι (the syntax is loose and possibly corrupt), ἔγχυμα, οὐδ’ αὐτούς, ὄγκου

How that man met me on these points, and gave no fitting answer, would be a task for much leisure, 561 and not a task proper to the matters now before us. Since I have no leisure now for idle talk, I decided not to do it now; rather, in my treatise on venesection I have resolved to set out all the indications of this remedy, which no one has written down with precision and with proper distinctions. Leaving aside for the present, then, the argument against the Empiricists, I return once more to the Rationalists, examining each of the symptoms they have written down. For some of them are signs of plethora relative to the faculty, others of plethora in respect of the contents of the vessels; some are signs not of plethora simply, but of plethora of a particular kind; and some are signs of no plethora at all, but either of cacochymia and corruption, or of a weak faculty, or of increased bulk of the solid bodies. That this is so you will learn if you attend to what is about to be said.

Greek (Kühn)

ἡ μὲν δὴ τονώδης διάθεσις, ὅταν ἄνευ γυμνασίων συμπίπτῃ, ἐνδείκνυται μὲν πάντως πολλὴν οὐσίαν ἐν αἰσθητικῷ σώματι περιέχεσθαι, οὐ μὴν ἥτις γέ ἐστιν ἡ οὐσία, ἢ τὸ εἶδος αὐτῆς, ἔτ’ ἐνδείκνυται, κατά γε τοῦτο μόνον αὐτὸ τὸ τείνεσθαι, κοινὸν 562 κοινὸν ὑπάρχον ἁπάσης οὐσίας, καὶ λεπτῆς, καὶ παχείας, καὶ μέσης τῇ συστάσει, καὶ πνευματώδους, ἔτι τε θερμῆς τε καὶ ψυχρᾶς, ἢ μέσης, ἀλλ’ ἑτέρων τινῶν δεῖται διαγνωρισμάτων εἰς τὸν τούτου διορισμόν. ἡ δὲ ἐν ταῖς ἐνεργείαις ἀτονία τὴν δύναμιν ἀῤῥωστοτέραν δείκνυσι, τῷ ἐπισχεῖν τὸ κινοῦν, ὡς ἐπὶ χειρῶν κινήσεως ἀσθενοῦς· πολλαῖς γὰρ ἀκολουθεῖ καταστάσεσιν· εἴτε γὰρ αὐξηθείη τινὶ τό τε σαρκῶδες καὶ τὸ πιμελῶδες γένος, ἐν τοῖς αὐτοῖς ὅροις διαμενούσης τῆς δυνάμεως, ἀνάγκη τὰς κινήσεις ἀτονωτέρας γίγνεσθαι, μένοντος μὲν ἴσου τοῦ κινοῦντος, αὐξηθέντος δὲ τοῦ κινουμένου· εἴτε τῶν χυμῶν τις εἷς, ἢ καὶ πάντες ἅμα πλεονάσειαν, μὴ συναυξηθείσης αὐτοῖς τῆς δυνάμεως, ἀνάγκη καὶ νῦν ἀτονωτέρας γενέσθαι τὰς ἐνεργείας· εἴτ’ ἄμφω μὲν συναυξηθείη, τά τε παρεγχύματα καλούμενα καὶ τὰ ὑγρὰ, μὴ συναυξηθείη δὲ ἡ δύναμις, εἴτε τούτων ὡσαύτως ἐχόντων, ἡ δύναμις ἀσθενεστέρα γένοιτο.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τῷ ἐπισχεῖν τὸ κινοῦν, κοινὸν κοινὸν

The tensive condition, then, whenever it occurs without exercises, indicates in every case that a large quantity of substance is contained in a sentient body; it does not, however, further indicate what the substance is, or its kind—at least in respect of this alone, namely the being stretched itself, which is common 562 to every substance, thin, thick, and intermediate in consistency, and pneumatic, and further hot and cold, or intermediate—but it requires certain other distinguishing signs for the determination of this. The atony in the activities, on the other hand, shows the faculty to be weaker, in that the mover is checked, as in the case of a weak movement of the hands; for it follows upon many states. For whether the fleshy and the fatty kind have been increased in someone, while the faculty remains within the same limits, it is necessary that the movements become more lacking in tone, since the mover remains equal while the moved has been increased; or whether some one of the humours, or indeed all together, are in excess, the faculty not having been increased along with them, in this case too it is necessary that the activities become more lacking in tone; or whether both have been increased together, both what are called the parenchymata and the fluids, but the faculty has not been increased along with them; or whether, these remaining as they were, the faculty has become weaker.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐν αἰσθητικῷ σώματι, τῷ ἐπισχεῖν τὸ κινοῦν, κοινὸν [562] κοινὸν (apparent dittography, translated once)

Now the tensive condition, when it occurs without exercises, indicates in every case that much substance is contained in a sentient body; it does not, however, go on to indicate which substance it is, or what its kind is, at least by this alone, the being stretched itself, which is common 562 to all substance: thin, thick, and intermediate in consistency, and pneumatic, and further hot and cold, or intermediate. Rather, it requires certain other distinguishing signs for determining this. Lack of tone in the activities, on the other hand, shows the faculty to be more infirm, in that the moving agent is held back, as in the case of a weak movement of the hands; for it follows upon many states. For if in someone both the fleshy and the fatty kind should be increased while the faculty remains within the same limits, the movements must become more lacking in tone, since the mover remains equal while what is moved has increased. Or if any one of the humours, or even all of them together, should be in excess while the faculty has not increased along with them, then too the activities must become more lacking in tone. Or if both should increase together, the so-called parenchymata and the fluids, but the faculty does not increase along with them; or if, these remaining in the same condition, the faculty should become weaker.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: θάτερον αὐτῶν ὁποτερονοῦν, ἀνάλογον ἡ πιμελὴ μᾶλλον αὔξεται, ἐν πιμελῇ μᾶλλον ἡ σὰρξ ἢ μόνη πιμελή

And it could sometimes happen that both come together, so that the faculty becomes weaker 563 and the humours together with the parenchymata are either more or fewer—either one of these two, whichever it may be; and on account of these things too the activities become weaker, each of them occurring singly, or two together, or more, or all at once. For to suppose that a weaker motion could ever be produced as a result of the good nourishment of the solid bodies belongs to men who do not recognise that the faculties extend through the whole of the solid bodies, but who set up the arteries and the nerves as pipes or conduits for pneuma. You will learn the faultiness of this opinion from many other sources, but not least also from two of our treatises, that On the Doctrines of Hippocrates and Plato and that On the Natural Faculties. Now the good nourishment of the solid bodies is what is proper to good condition (euexia), whereas the increase of the fleshes is proper to good-fleshedness (eusarkia) and much-fleshedness (polysarkia). For the most part, then, the fat increases more, in proportion; but sometimes the flesh increases more within fat (ἐν πιμελῇ), or fat alone. These matters have been discussed in the commentaries On Mixtures.

Greek (Kühn)

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And just as in the case of flesh there is on the one hand good-fleshedness (eusarkia), on the other much-fleshedness (polysarkia), 564 in the same way in the case of blood there is on the one hand what is, as it were, a kind of good-bloodedness (euaimia), and on the other much-bloodedness (polyaimia). Now the condition of good-bloodedness is clearly, in both quality and quantity, exactly according to nature; whereas that of much-bloodedness is blameless in respect of quality, but in respect of quantity not blameless, but in every case more, either than what exists according to nature from birth, or than what is in proportion to the faculty. For it is possible at times for both to increase together with one another beyond the nature from birth, both the strength of the faculty and the quantity of blood, as in the athletic good conditions (euexiai); for indeed the moist and the fleshy parts and the faculty increase together in proportion to one another in them. And so it is now evident that those who have declared the bulk of the body to be one of the plethoric signs do not speak the truth either; for bulk of the veins would be a plethoric sign, but bulk of flesh and fat would be a plethora of those very things, not however that which we are now investigating, which is indeed also called plethora.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τοῦ πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν, ἐκείνων ἂν εἴη πλῆθος αὐτῶν, τὸ δὲ τῆς πολυαιμίας

Just as in flesh there is on the one hand good flesh, on the other abundance of flesh, 564 in the same way in blood there is on the one hand something like a good blood, on the other an abundance of blood. Now as regards good blood, it is not unclear that it is exactly in accordance with nature both in quality and in quantity; that of abundance of blood, however, is blameless in its quality but not blameless in its quantity: it is in every case greater, either than what is in accordance with nature from birth, or than what stands in relation to the capacity. For it is possible at times for both to increase together with one another beyond the nature present from birth, both the strength of the capacity and the quantity of the blood, as in the athletic states of good condition; for in these the moist and the fleshy parts and the capacity increase together in proportion with one another. And so it is by now quite plain that those who have declared the bulk of the body to be one of the plethoric signs do not speak truly either. For bulk of the veins would be a plethoric sign, but bulk of the flesh and of the fat would be an abundance of those things themselves, not, however, the abundance we are now investigating, which is indeed also called plethora.

Greek (Kühn)

περὶ γὰρ τούτου τοῦ πλήθους, ὃ δηλονότι κένωσιν ἐνδείκνυται, προθέμενοι λέγειν ἐς τοσοῦτον, ὅ ποτε 565 ὁμωνυμίαις ἐνδείκνυται, ἐσφάλησαν ἔνιοί τινες ἐξ αὐτῶν, ὥστε εἰς τὸ τῶν σαρκῶν τε καὶ τῆς πιμελῆς ἀπενεχθῆναι πλῆθος. οὕτω μὲν δὴ μοχθηρὸν πλήθους γνώρισμα, σώματος ὄγκος· αὐτῶν μὲν γὰρ ἂν ἐκείνων εἴη τῶν φλεβῶν ὧν ὁρῶμεν ἐνδεικτικὸς τῆς πληρώσεως, οὐ μὴν ἐξ ἀνάγκης γε τῶν ἐν τῷ βάθει· ἀλλ’ ἐνδέχεταί ποτε καὶ τῶν ἔξω φλεβῶν διατεταμένων οὐχ ὡσαύτως διατετάσθαι καὶ τὰς ἔνδον, οἷον αὐτίκα κρύους μὲν κατασχόντος, εἰς τὸ βάθος ἀπελαύνεται τὸ αἷμα, καὶ λειφαιμοῦσι μὲν οὕτω δὴ αἱ ἐκτὸς, ὡς μηδὲ φαίνεσθαι· πάλιν δὲ ἀλέας γενομένης, ἢ λουσαμένων, ἐπὶ τὸ δέρμα καὶ τὰ ἐκτὸς τοῦ σώματος φέρεται τὸ αἷμα, καταλιπὸν τὸ βάθος· εἰ δὲ καὶ περικαὴς εἴη πυρετὸς, ἀναγκαῖον καὶ τότε καίεσθαι τῷ σώματι, καθάπερ ζέοντι, πνευματοῦσθαί τε καὶ χεῖσθαι. ὥστε ὁ τῶν φλεβῶν ὄγκος οὐκ ἔστιν ἀχώριστον γνώρισμα πλήθους χυμῶν, ἀλλὰ πρῶτον μὲν χρὴ τοιαύτην ὑπάρχειν τῷ σώματι κατάστασιν, ὡς μήτε τὰ ἔνδον τῶν ἔξω μήτε ἐκείνων ταῦτα πλεονεκτεῖν θερμότητι· δεύτερον δὲ, μηδ’ ἄμετρον εἶναι καθ’ ὅλον τὸ ζῶον θερμότητα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὅ ποτε ὁμωνυμίαις ἐνδείκνυται, καίεσθαι τῷ σώματι, καθάπερ ζέοντι

For having set out to speak about this plethos, which evidently indicates evacuation, some among them went astray to such an extent—a thing which homonymies at times bring about (ὅ ποτε ὁμωνυμίαις ἐνδείκνυται)—565 as to be carried off to the plethos of flesh and of fat. Thus, then, bulk of the body is a bad sign of plethos; for it would be indicative of the filling of those veins themselves which we see, but not necessarily of those in the depth; rather, it is possible at times, even when the outer veins are distended, that the inner ones are not distended in the same way; for example, straightaway when cold takes hold, the blood is driven into the depth, and the outer veins are so drained of blood as not even to be visible; and again, when warmth arises, or when people have bathed, the blood is carried to the skin and the outer parts of the body, abandoning the depth; and if there should also be a burning fever, it is necessary then too that the blood be burnt in the body, as though it were boiling, and be turned into pneuma and diffused. So that bulk of the veins is not an inseparable sign of a plethos of humours; rather, first there must be such a condition in the body that neither the inner parts exceed the outer nor the latter the former in heat; and second, that there not be immoderate heat throughout the whole animal.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐς τοσοῦτον, ὅ ποτε ὁμωνυμίαις ἐνδείκνυται, ἔνιοί τινες ἐξ αὐτῶν, καίεσθαι τῷ σώματι, πνευματοῦσθαί, χεῖσθαι

For, having set out to speak about this plethora, which evidently indicates evacuation, to so great an extent, as it is sometimes 565 indicated by homonymies, some of them went astray, so as to be carried off to the plethora of the flesh and of the fat. So poor a sign of plethora, then, is bulk of the body. Bulk of the veins, for its part, would be indicative of the filling of those very veins that we see, but not necessarily of those in the depth. Rather, it is possible at times that, even when the outer veins are distended, the inner ones are not likewise distended. For instance, when cold has taken hold, the blood is driven into the depth, and the outer veins become so deficient in blood that they do not even appear. Again, when there is warmth, or when people have bathed, the blood is carried to the skin and the outer parts of the body, leaving the depth behind. And if there should be a burning fever, it is necessary that then too it be burnt in the body, as though boiling, and be turned into pneuma and diffused. So bulk of the veins is not an inseparable sign of a plethora of humours. Rather, first, the body must be in such a condition that neither the inner parts exceed the outer, nor the outer the inner, in heat. Second, the heat throughout the whole animal must not be immoderate.

566 Once these things have been determined in advance, the swelling of the veins might on occasion be a sign of the sanguineous plethora now under discussion, that which is such by predominance; for it is impossible that blood so exact should ever be contained in the veins that nothing either of bile, or of phlegm, or of serous ichor is carried along in it, which is why in what preceded too I asked that it be understood in the same way, whether I should say that the humours are in excess or the blood.
