Greek (Kühn)

χρὴ δὲ μηδὲ κατάῤῥοπον ἐσχηματίσθαι τὸ τοιοῦτον μόριον, ἢ κεκινῆσθαι προσφάτως ὑπὲρ τὰ ἄλλα· διὸ καί τινι λόγῳ περιλαβὼν ἄν τις ἅπαντας τοὺς κατὰ μέρος διορισμοὺς, τότ’ ἐνδείκνυσθαι φήσει τὸν ὄγκον τῶν φλεβῶν αἵματος πλῆθος, ὅταν ἅπαντα τὰ μόρια τὴν αὐτὴν κατάστασιν ἔχῃ μετὰ συμμέτρου θερμασίας, ὡς εἴγε ὡσαύτως μὲν ἅπαντα διακέοιτο, θερμότης δὲ ἄμετρος ὑπάρχοι, χύσις ἐν τῷδε γίγνεται τῶν χυμῶν εἰς ὄγκον αἴρουσα τὰς φλέβας, οὐ μὴν αἱματικόν γε τὸ πλῆθος, ἀλλὰ πνευματικὸν ἐνδείκνυται· πνευματοῦνται γὰρ οἱ χυμοὶ θερμαινόμενοι, διὸ καὶ ὅσοι τὸν ὄγκον τῶν φλεβῶν ἀπεφήναντο πλῆθος σημαίνειν, ἐὰν δὴ προσυπακούσῃ τις ἐν τῷ λόγῳ τὸ κατ’ ἐκείνας τὰς φλέβας, ἐν αἷσπερ ἂν ᾖ τοῦτο γινόμενον, ὀρθῶς εἰρηκέναι δόξουσι· πλείων γὰρ ἡ οὐσία τῆς πρόσθεν ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἐστὶν ἡ τὸν ὄγκον ἐργασαμένη ταῖς φλεψὶν, οὐ μὴν ἤδη γέ πω δῆλον, ὁποῖόν τι τὸ πλῆθός ﻿ 568 ἐστιν· ἁπάσης γὰρ οὐσίας πολλῆς ὁ παρὰ φύσιν ὄγκος κοινὸς, εἴτ’ οὖν αἷμα καθαρὸν, εἴτε χολῶδες, εἴτε ὀῤῥῶδες, εἴτε φλεγματικὸν, εἴτε πνευματικὸν ὑπάρχοι. ὥσπερ δὲ τοῦτο κοινὸν ἁπάσης πλήθους διαφορᾶς, οὕτω καὶ τὸ τείνεσθαι τὰς φλέβας· ἕπεται γὰρ καὶ τοῦτο τῷ ποσῷ τοῦ πλήθους, οὐ τῇ κατ’ εἶδος διαφορᾷ. ὥστε ὁ ὄγκος καὶ ἡ τονώδης αἴσθησις οὐ μᾶλλόν τι χυμῶν ἢ πνεύματος ἐνδείξεται πλῆθος. εἰ μὲν οὖν τις ὧδέ πως ἀποφαίνοιτο, κατὰ τὰς τονώδεις αἰσθήσεις ἐξ ἀνάγκης πλῆθός ἐστιν, ἀληθὲς ἐρεῖ· καὶ μέντοι κᾂν οὕτως φήσειε, τὸ ἐν αἰσθητικοῖς μέρεσι πλῆθος ἐπιφέρει τινὰ διάθεσιν, ἀληθὲς ἐρεῖ καὶ αὐτός· εἰ δέ τις ἐάσας τὸ γενικὸν ὄνομα τὸ πλῆθος ἢ τοὺς χυμοὺς, αἷμα παραλάβοι κατὰ τὸν λόγον, οὐκ ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἀληθεύσει δι’ ἃς εἴπομεν αἰτίας. ὅτι δ’ οὐδὲ πλήθους ἁπλῶς, ἀλλὰ τοῦ τε μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον ἐν αὐτῷ, διαφορὰ κατὰ τε τοὺς ὄγκους ἐστὶ τῶν φλεβῶν καὶ τὴν τονώδη διάθεσιν, ἐνθένδε δῆλον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τινὰ διάθεσιν

And such a part must neither have been positioned in a downward-sloping posture, nor have been recently moved more than the others. Hence, too, one who embraced all the particular distinctions in a single formula would say that the swelling of the veins indicates a plethora of blood when all the parts have the same condition together with a well-proportioned heat; since if all were disposed alike, but the heat were immoderate, in this case there occurs a diffusion of the humours which raises the veins into swelling, and yet it indicates not a sanguineous plethora but a pneumatic one; for the humours are turned into pneuma when heated. Hence also those who declared that the swelling of the veins signifies plethora will seem to have spoken correctly, provided one supplies in addition in the statement 'in those veins in which this happens to be occurring'; for the substance that has produced the swelling in the veins is necessarily greater than before; yet it is not yet at all clear what sort of thing the plethora 568 is; for the swelling contrary to nature is common to every abundant substance, whether it be pure blood, or bilious, or serous, or phlegmatic, or pneumatic. And just as this is common to every variety of plethora, so too is the stretching of the veins; for this too follows upon the quantity of the plethora, not upon the difference in kind. Consequently the swelling and the tensive sensation will indicate a plethora of humours no more than one of pneuma. If, then, someone were to declare in some such way as this, 'in the case of tensive sensations there is necessarily plethora', he will speak the truth; and indeed even if he were to say thus, 'plethora in sensitive parts brings on a certain disposition', he too will speak the truth; but if someone, abandoning the generic name 'plethora' or 'humours', were to take 'blood' into the statement, he will not necessarily speak the truth, for the reasons we have stated. And that there is a difference, in the swellings of the veins and in the tensive disposition, not of plethora simply, but of the more and the less in it, is clear from the following.

Greek (Kühn)

οὐδὲν μόριον ὀδυνᾶται πληρούμενον, ἄνευ τοῦ προγενέσθαι τάσιν αὐτῷ βιαίαν· 569 εἰ δ’ εἰς ὄγκον μὲν αἴροιτο, μηδέπω δέ γε βιαίως, οὐδέπω τείνεται· διὸ τῶν ὄγκων ὁ μὲν σὺν τῇ τάσει πλέον ἐνδείξεται πλῆθος· ὁ δ’ ἄνευ ταύτης, ἧττον. πῶς οὖν ἐνίοτε τὴν ἀρχὴν μὲν οὐδεὶς ὄγκος φαίνεται κατὰ τὰς φλέβας, ἡ τονώδης δέ ἐστιν ἰσχυρὰ αἴσθησις; χρὴ γὰρ δήπου τοῖς μεγίστοις ὄγκοις ἐζεῦχθαι τὴν τοῦ τείνεσθαι διάθεσιν. ἢ διότι ἔστιν ὅτε ὁ ὄγκος ἐν ταῖς διὰ βάθους φλεψὶ μόναις διατείνει, ἢ εἴπερ ἐν τῷ πρὸς τὶ τὸ πλῆθος, ὑπὲρ οὗ νῦν δια λεγόμεθα, τό τε πρὸς τὴν εὐρύτητα τῶν ἀγγείων, ὃ δὴ πλῆθος λέγομεν, οὐδέπω τεινομένων αὐτῶν, τό τε πρὸς τὴν τοῦ χιτῶνος τάσιν, δηλονότι τοῦ μὲν ὄγκου τῶν φλεβῶν ἡ γένεσις ἐπιδίδοται, εἰς πλῆθος αὐξομένου τοῦ αἵματος ὑπὲρ τὴν φυσικὴν ἑκάστου συμμετρίαν, ὥστε πρὸς ἐκείνην λέγεσθαι τὸ πολύ· τὸ δὲ τείνεσθαι τὸν χιτῶνα τῆς ὡς πρὸς αὐτὸν συμμετρίας ἐνδείκνυται (διὰ) ὑπερβολήν. οὐ γὰρ ἔστι κατὰ φύσιν οὐδὲ τοῦτον τείνεσθαι. ἑκάτερον οὖν ἐστι πολὺ, τό τε ὡς πρὸς τὴν κατὰ φύσιν συμμετρίαν αἷμα καὶ πρὸς τὴν τοῦ χιτῶνος τάσιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: (διὰ), ἐπιδίδοται

No part suffers pain when it is being filled, without a violent tension having first come about in it; 569 but if it is raised into a swelling, yet not yet violently, it is not yet stretched. Hence, among swellings, the one accompanied by tension will indicate a greater plethora, the one without it, a lesser. How then is it that sometimes no swelling at all appears in the veins, while the tensive sensation is strong? For surely the condition of being stretched ought to be yoked to the greatest swellings. Either it is because there are times when the swelling distends only the veins in the depth, or, if indeed the plethora about which we are now discoursing belongs among things relative to something—both that relative to the width of the vessels, which we in fact call plethora, when they are not yet being stretched, and that relative to the tension of the coat—then clearly the genesis of the swelling of the veins advances as the blood increases into plethora beyond the natural due proportion of each, so that 'much' is said relative to that; whereas the coat's being stretched indicates (διὰ) an excess over the due proportion relative to the coat itself. For it is not in accordance with nature that this too should be stretched. Each, then, is 'much': both the blood relative to the natural due proportion and that relative to the tension of the coat.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἢ εἴπερ ἐν τῷ πρὸς τὶ τὸ πλῆθος, ἐπιδίδοται, (διὰ) ὑπερβολήν, τῆς ὡς πρὸς αὐτὸν συμμετρίας

No part feels pain while it is being filled unless a violent stretching (τάσις) has first come about in it; 569 but if it should be raised into a swelling, yet not yet violently, it is not yet stretched. Therefore, of swellings, the one accompanied by stretching will indicate a greater plethora, and the one without it a lesser. How, then, is it that sometimes no swelling at all appears in the veins at the outset, and yet the tensive sensation is strong? For surely the condition of being stretched ought to be yoked to the very great swellings. Either it is because the swelling sometimes distends only the veins that lie in the depth; or else, if within plethora taken in relation to something, about which we are now discoursing, there is both the plethora in relation to the width of the vessels, which we call plethora, while they are not yet stretched, and the plethora in relation to the stretching of the coat, then clearly the generation of the swelling of the veins advances (ἐπιδίδοται) as the blood increases to a plethora beyond the natural due proportion of each, so that 'much' is said in relation to that proportion; whereas the stretching of the coat indicates an excess beyond the due proportion relative to the coat itself. For it is not natural for this, either, to be stretched. Each, then, is 'much': both the blood taken in relation to the natural due proportion, and the blood taken in relation to the stretching of the coat.

Erasistratus made mention of both, 570 not in an articulated way, nor so clearly as I do now, yet in such a way as to make plain that the nature of the matter is twofold. For in saying, "At the beginning, then, and when the vessels are moderately filled, they appear more well-toned and stronger than their usual selves, beyond what is customary," he indicated the disproportion of the blood beyond what is according to nature for each individual; but in what he adds next, saying, "but when they come to a greater degree of filling, the upper arms and hands and shins become filled out," he indicated that disproportion which is relative to the coats that contain the blood; so that there are two limits of the increase of the blood beyond what is according to nature: one consisting in the coats of the veins being filled, raised into a swelling beyond what is according to nature for each individual; the other, in which, in addition to this, it comes about that they are stretched—neither of these being seen in the plethora relative to the faculty. On these matters, then, this too is sufficient. Redness, which they also mention, is a sign not of plethora simply, but of sanguineous plethora.

Erasistratus mentioned both, 570 not in an articulated way, it is true, nor as clearly as I do now, but nevertheless so as to make plain that the nature of the matter is twofold. For in saying: 'At the beginning, then, when the vessels are filled moderately, they appear more vigorous and stronger than themselves, beyond what is customary', he indicated the disproportion of the blood in excess of what is natural for each person; and in what he adds next, saying: 'but when they come further into filling, the arms and hands and shins are filled up as well', he indicated the disproportion relative to the coats that contain the blood. About these matters, then, this too is sufficient. Redness, which they also mention, is a sign not of plethora simply, but of sanguine plethora.

And it too will require the same distinctions as the swelling does; 571 for if it is due to some external cause, such as sun, or a bath, or exercise, or a walk, or, to put it simply, heat—and likewise also anger, shame and all such things; and acute fevers too are of this class. For such a colour also accompanies inflammations, whether they form in the muscles or in the skins, since they are plethoric diseases. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὡς εἰ καὶ ὁπότερον ἂν καταληφθείη μέρος

But we do not say that plethos is constituted in the whole body 572 in such a way that both kinds of bodies have been harmed, both that of the vessels and that of the fleshes; for it is possible sometimes for only one of the two to be so, as would be the case if whichever part were seized, this happening not in some one part or two, but either throughout the whole animal or in the most numerous and the most principal parts. And so, to discover what sort of thing the bulk is requires a man sufficiently exercised in diagnoses. In the fleshy kind, at any rate, one bulk is that of the fleshes themselves, another that of the humours, the flesh being filled, like some wool or a sponge, with superfluous moisture; but such a plethos in the fleshes is analogous to that in the vessels which is called plethos in respect of the filling, while the other plethos is analogous to that in relation to the faculty, or with heaviness—we bearing in mind that it makes no difference whether one discourses about composite flesh or about muscles, and that if ever the discussion should come to be about the plethos in the fleshes, one must understand the muscles, not that simple flesh which is named by some parenchyma.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς εἰ καὶ ὁπότερον ἂν καταληφθείη μέρος, ὄγκος, πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν

But we do not say that abundance arises in the whole body in such a way that both kinds of bodies are harmed, that of the vessels and that of the fleshes. For it is possible that sometimes only one of the two is affected, so that whichever of the two parts is seized, this has occurred not in some one or two parts but either throughout the whole animal or in the most numerous and most principal parts. And accordingly, to discover of what sort the swelling (ὄγκος) is also requires a man sufficiently trained in diagnoses. In the fleshy kind, at any rate, one swelling is that of the fleshes themselves, another that of the fluids, when the flesh, like a piece of wool or a sponge, has been filled with superfluous moisture. But this kind of abundance in the fleshes is analogous to what is called, in the case of the vessels, abundance with respect to the infused content (enchyma), while the other abundance is analogous to that relative to the faculty (δύναμις), or accompanied by heaviness. We must remember that it makes no difference whether one speaks of composite flesh or of muscles, and that whenever the discussion is about the abundance in the fleshes, one must understand the muscles, not that simple flesh which is named parenchyma by some.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

In the arteries and the veins, however, the plethora relative to the faculty is not 573 similarly perceptible to the patients themselves; rather, only in those who are by nature most sensitive does a certain faint and deep-seated heaviness make itself apparent throughout the body. Therefore one must seek certain other, clearer indications of such plethora. But since most physicians have been negligent about this matter, some not having investigated it at all and some only deficiently, no more secure indication has been found than the motion of the arteries, which we have expounded sufficiently in the treatises on the pulses. For in most cases the veins come into a state of plethora together with the arteries, on account of the confluence of the vessels, which we have often demonstrated elsewhere; but sometimes, when viscous or thick humours have accumulated in the veins, their transfer into the arteries does not come about easily — though such a case is rare; for unless the humours are extremely thick or viscous, they will in any case flow across.

In the arteries and veins, however, 573 the plethora relative to the strength is not perceptible in the same way to the patients themselves; rather, only to those who are by nature most sensitive does a faint and deep-seated heaviness appear in the body. But since most physicians have been negligent about it, some not having examined it at all and others only deficiently, no surer sign has been found than the motion of the arteries, which we have expounded sufficiently in the treatises on the pulses. For the most part the veins come into a state of plethora together with the arteries, on account of the confluence of the vessels, which we have often demonstrated in other works; but sometimes, when viscous or thick humours have collected in the veins, their transfer into the arteries does not take place readily. Such a thing, however, is rare; for unless the humours are extremely thick or viscous, they will certainly flow across.

ἐν μὲν δὴ ταῖς τοιαύταις διαθέσεσιν οὐδὲν ἐναργὲς γνώρισμα τοῦ κατὰ τὰς φλέβας ἠθροισμένου πλήθους ἐκ (δὲ) τῆς τῶν ἀρτηριῶν κινήσεως ἔνεστι λαβεῖν, εἰ καὶ ὅτι μάλιστα τῷ Νικάρχῳ καὶ Πραξαγόρᾳ δοκεῖ, καίτοι μὴ μεταδιδοῦσι 574 ταῖς ἀρτηρίαις αἵματος· κατὰ μέντοι τὰς ἄλλας διαθέσεις τῆς πληθώρας πιστεύειν χρὴ τοῖς ἐκ τῶν σφυγμῶν σημείοις, ἃ διὰ τῆς οἰκείας εἴρηται πραγματείας, τὰς δὲ ἰδέας τοῦ πλήθους, ὅταν γε ὁμαλῶς ἐκτεταμένον εἰς ὅλον τὸ σῶμα ᾖ, κατὰ τάδε διαιρεῖσθαι. ἐξέρυθρον μὲν γὰρ τὸ τοῦ αἵματος πλῆθός ἐστιν, ὕπωχρον δὲ τὸ τῆς ξανθῆς χολῆς, ἐπὶ δὲ τὸ λευκότερον ἐκτρέπεται τοῦ κατὰ φύσιν, ὅταν τὸ φλέγμα κρατῇ, καθάπερ ἐπὶ τὸ μελάντερον, ἢν ἡ μέλαινα χολή. καθ’ ἕκαστον δὲ τῶν εἰρημένων συνηυξῆσθαι χρὴ καὶ τὸ αἷμα· μόνη γὰρ εἴ ποτε πλεονάσειεν ἡ ὠχρὰ καὶ ξανθὴ προσαγορευομένη χολὴ, καθάπερ ἐν ἰκτερικοῖς, οὐκ ἔτι πλῆθος, οὐδὲ πληθωρικὴ διάθεσις, ἀλλὰ κακοχυμία τὸ τοιοῦτόν ἐστι πάθημα. κατὰ ταὐτὰ δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ τῆς μελαίνης χολῆς καὶ τοῦ φλέγματος. ὅταν δὲ, ὡς εἴρηται πρόσθεν, ἢ βάρος, ἢ ὄγκος ὑπὲρ τὸ κατὰ φύσιν ᾖ, πληθωρικὴ μὲν ἡ τοιαύτη διάθεσις εἴρηται, ἐκ δὲ τῆς χρόας γινώσκειν χρὴ τὴν ἰδέαν τοῦ κρατοῦντος χυμοῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐκ (δὲ) τῆς τῶν ἀρτηριῶν κινήσεως

In such conditions, then, no clear sign of the plethos gathered in the veins can be obtained from the movement of the arteries, however much Nicarchus and Praxagoras may think so, even though they do not grant 574 the arteries any share of blood. In the other conditions of plethora, however, one must trust the signs drawn from the pulses, which have been set out in their proper treatise, and distinguish the forms of plethos, at least when it is evenly extended throughout the whole body, in the following way. For the plethos of blood is deep red, that of yellow bile somewhat pale-yellow; and the colour turns toward whiter than the natural when phlegm predominates, just as toward darker if black bile does. In each of the cases mentioned the blood too must have increased along with it; for if the bile called pale-yellow and yellow should ever be in excess by itself, as in icteric patients, such an affection is no longer plethos, nor a plethoric condition, but cacochymia. The same holds also in the case of black bile and phlegm. But when, as was said earlier, there is either heaviness or bulk beyond what is natural, such a condition has been called plethoric, and from the colour one must recognise the form of the predominant humour.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκ (δὲ) — Kühn's bracketed δὲ makes no sense in the construction and is left untranslated, καίτοι μὴ μεταδιδοῦσι ταῖς ἀρτηρίαις αἵματος — the dative participle is taken as agreeing with Nicarchus and Praxagoras ('although they do not give the arteries a share of blood'); it could perhaps refer to the veins not passing blood to the arteries, ὕπωχρον, ὠχρὰ, ἐξέρυθρον

In such conditions, then, it is not possible to obtain any clear sign of the plenitude collected in the veins from the movement of the arteries, however much this may seem to be so to Nicarchus and Praxagoras, although they do not give the arteries 574 a share of blood. In the other conditions of plethora, however, one must trust the signs from the pulses, which have been stated in the treatise proper to them. The kinds of plenitude, at least when it is evenly extended into the whole body, one must distinguish as follows. The plenitude of blood is deep red; that of yellow bile is somewhat pale-yellow; it turns towards what is whiter than the natural state when phlegm prevails, just as it turns towards what is darker if black bile prevails. In each of the cases mentioned the blood too must have increased along with it. For if ever the bile called pale-yellow and yellow should be in excess by itself alone, as in the jaundiced, such an affection is no longer plenitude, nor a plethoric condition, but cacochymia. The same holds for black bile and for phlegm. But when, as was said before, there is either heaviness or bulk beyond the natural state, such a condition is called plethoric, and one must recognise from the colour the kind of the prevailing humour.

Greek (Kühn)

μόνη δὲ χολὴ τῶν ἀχωρίστων τοῦ πλήθους 575 συμπτωμάτων οὐδέτερον ἐργάσασθαι δύναται, οὔτ’ οὖν τὸν ὄγκον οὔτε τὸ βάρος· ἕτερον γάρ τι πρὸς ταύτης ἔσται πάθημα πρὶν ἐς τοσοῦτον αὐξηθῆναι. μόνον δ’, εἴπερ ἄρα, τὸ φλέγμα, καὶ τοῦτο οὐχ ἅπαν ἁπλῶς, ἀλλὰ τὸ κατὰ τοὺς λευκοφλεγματίας ὀνομαζομένους ὑδέρους ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ σώματι δυνήσεται πλεονάσαι. δῆλον δὲ ὡς καὶ τὸν ὠμὸν ὀνομαζόμενον χυμὸν ἕν τι τῶν φλέγματος εἰδῶν ἔν γε τῷ νῦν λόγῳ τίθεσθαι χρὴ, ὡς εἴγε ἕτερον ἐκείνου θήσομεν πρὸς αἷς εἴπομεν ἄρτι πλήθους διαφοραῖς, ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστι προσθεῖναι καὶ τὴν ἐκ τοῦ τοιούτου χυμοῦ· παχύτερος δὲ τοῦ ἄλλου φλέγματος ὁ χυμὸς οὗτος, ἧττόν τε πνευματικὸς, οἷος ἐν τοῖς οὔροις ὑφίσταται μά λιστα τοῖς ἐξ ἀδδηφαγίας πολλὴν ὑπόστασιν ἔτνει τε κυάμων ὁμοίαν ἔχουσι· καὶ μὴν καὶ διεχώρησε κάτω πολλάκις ὁ τοιοῦτος ἀθρόως χυμὸς ἐκείνοις τῶν σωμάτων, ὅσα γυμνασίων μὲν ἀπείχετο, παμπόλλοις δὲ ἐδέσμασιν ἐχρᾶτο, καὶ λουτροῖς ἐπὶ τροφαῖς· ἀλλὰ καὶ τούτῳ τῷ χυμῷ, καθάπερ καὶ τῷ φλέγματι, λευκή τις ἄχροια καθ’ ὅλον τὸ σῶμα συνυπάρχει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Bile alone can produce neither of the inseparable symptoms of plethora 575 — neither, then, the swelling nor the heaviness; for some other affection will arise from it before it is increased to so great a degree. Only phlegm, if indeed anything, and this not every phlegm simply, but that found in the dropsies called leucophlegmatic, will be able to abound in the whole body. And it is clear that the humour called crude also must, in the present discussion at least, be set down as one of the kinds of phlegm; since if we posit it as something distinct from that, it is necessary to add, to the differentiae of plethora which we mentioned just now, also the one arising from such a humour. This humour is thicker than the other phlegm and less pneumatic, such as settles in the urines, especially in those of people who, from gluttony, have a copious sediment resembling bean-soup; and indeed such a humour has often passed downward all at once in those bodies which abstained from exercises but made use of very many foods, and of baths after meals; but with this humour too, just as with phlegm, a certain white pallor coexists throughout the whole body.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πρὸς ταύτης, μά λιστα (printed split; read μάλιστα), ἀδδηφαγίας (for ἀδηφαγίας), ἔχουσι (referring to the urines or to the patients), ἔτνει κυάμων, ἄχροια

But bile alone can produce neither of the symptoms that are inseparable from plethora 575, neither the bulk nor the heaviness; for some other affection will be produced by it before it has increased to so great an extent. Phlegm alone, if indeed any humour, and not all phlegm without qualification, but that which occurs in the dropsies called leukophlegmatiai, will be able to be in excess throughout the whole body. It is clear that the so-called raw humour too must, at least in the present discussion, be counted as one of the kinds of phlegm; for if we shall posit it as different from that, then in addition to the differences of plethora we have just stated it is necessary to add also the one arising from such a humour. This humour is thicker than the other phlegm and less pneumatic, such as settles in urines, especially those which, as a result of gluttony, have a copious sediment resembling bean-pottage. Moreover, such a humour has often passed downwards all at once in those bodies that abstained from exercises but made use of very many foods, and of baths after meals. But with this humour too, as with phlegm, a certain white discoloration is present together with it throughout the whole body.

Greek (Kühn)

τὰ δ’ ἄλλα τοῦ φλέγματος εἴδη τὸ μέν 576 ἐστι γλυκὺ, τὸ δὲ ὀξὺ, τὸ δὲ ἁλυκόν· καὶ ὑπνώδεις μὲν γίνονται ἐπὶ τοῦ γλυκέος, πεινώδεις δὲ ἐπὶ τοῦ ὀξέος πλεονάζοντος, ὥσπερ δὴ διψώδεις, ὅταν ἐπικρατῇ τὸ ἁλυκόν· ἀνόρεκτοι δὲ ἐπὶ τῷ προειρημένῳ τῷ ὠμῷ. τὰ δὲ τῶν ὕπνων ἐπὶ μὲν τοῖς ἄλλοις φλέγμασι τοῖς προειρημένοις βραχὺ πλείω τοῦ κατὰ φύσιν· ὁ δ’ ὠμὸς χυμὸς, ὥσπερ τὸ γλυκὺ φλέγμα, σαφῶς ὑπνώδεις ἀποτελεῖ· δέδεικται γὰρ ἑτέρωθι, τῆς αἰσθητικῆς ἀρχῆς ὑγραινομένης τε καὶ ψυχομένης, τὴν κωματώδη ἕπεσθαι διάθεσιν, ὥσπέρ γε καὶ τὴν ἀγρυπνίαν, ξηραινομένης καὶ θερμαινομένης. ταῦτ’ ἄρα καὶ οἱ χολῶντες ἄγρυπνοι πάντες ἐφ’ ἑκατέρᾳ τῇ χολῇ· σύνεστι δὲ ἐξαίρετον ἐπὶ τῆς μελαίνης τὸ σκυθρωπόν τε καὶ δύσθυμον ἄνευ λόγου. εἰ δὲ μὴ καθ’ ὅλον σῶμα πλεονάζοιεν οἱ προειρημένοι χυμοὶ, καθ’ ἕν τι δὲ μόριον ἀθροισθεῖεν, ὄγκους ἐργάζονται παρὰ φύσιν αὐτοῖς τοῖς σώμασιν, ὑπὲρ ὧν ἑτέρωθι δείκνυται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

As for the other kinds of phlegm, one 576 is sweet, another sour, another salty; and people become somnolent when the sweet kind is in excess, hungry when the sour kind is in excess, just as they become thirsty when the salty kind prevails; and they become without appetite with the aforementioned raw humour. As regards sleep, in the case of the other phlegms mentioned above it is a little more than what is in accordance with nature; but the raw humour, like the sweet phlegm, clearly renders people somnolent. For it has been shown elsewhere that when the sensory principle is moistened and cooled, the comatose condition follows, just as sleeplessness also follows when it is dried and heated. For this reason, then, those who suffer from bile are all sleepless, with either of the two biles; and in the case of the black bile there is present in addition, as a distinctive feature, sullenness and despondency without reason. But if the aforementioned humours should not be in excess throughout the whole body, but be gathered together in some one part, they produce swellings contrary to nature in those very bodies, concerning which demonstration is given elsewhere.

Of the other kinds of phlegm, one 576 is sweet, another sour, another salty. People become sleepy with the sweet, hungry when the sour is in excess, just as they become thirsty when the salty predominates; and they are without appetite with the raw humour mentioned before. As for sleep, with the other phlegms mentioned before it is a little more than accords with nature; but the raw humour, like the sweet phlegm, plainly makes people sleepy. For it has been shown elsewhere that when the sensory principle is moistened and cooled, the comatose condition follows, just as sleeplessness does when it is dried and heated. For this reason also those who suffer from bile are all sleepless, with either bile; but peculiar to the black bile there is present, besides, sullenness and despondency without reason. If the humours mentioned before are not in excess throughout the whole body but are collected in some one part, they produce swellings contrary to nature in the bodies themselves, about which it is shown elsewhere.

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ αὐτὰ δὲ τὰ νῦν αὐτῶν εἰρημένα συμπτώματα, πλὴν τῆς ἀχροίας, ἐγκέφαλόν τε καὶ στόμαχον 577 κατειληφότων ἐστίν· ἀγρυπνίαι μὲν γὰρ καὶ ὕπνοι διὰ τὸν ἐγκέφαλον, ὀρέξεις δὲ καὶ ἀνορεξίαι διὰ τὸν στόμαχον ἄλλοτε ἀλλοίως διατιθέμενον γίγνονται. δῆλον δ’ ὅτι τὸ στόμα τῆς γαστρὸς εἴρηται νῦν στόμαχος, ἐν ᾧ τὸ μὲν φλέγμα τὸ ἀθροισθὲν, ὡς ὀλίγον ἔμπροσθεν εἴρηται, τὰς ὀρέξεις ἐποίησε· τῶν δὲ χολωδῶν, ἐπὶ μὲν τῆς ξανθῆς διψώδεις τε καὶ ἀσώδεις ἔσονται, καὶ τὸ στόμα πικρὸν αὐτοῖς ἐστιν· ἐπὶ δὲ τῇ μελαίνῃ, δύσθυμοι, καὶ σκυθρωποὶ, καὶ φοβεροὶ, καὶ ἄδιψοι, καὶ σιέλου μεστοὶ, ἀπόσιτοι δὲ οὐ πάντες, ἀλλά τινες αὐτῶν. ἱκανῶς δὲ πεινώδεις εἰσὶν οἷς ἂν ἡ μέλαινα τὴν ὀξεῖαν ποιότητα κρατοῦσαν ἔχῃ· γίγνεται δὲ τοῦτο, κακοηθεστέρας αὐτῆς ἀποτελουμένης. ἔστ’ ἂν δὲ μόνον οἷον ἰλὺς αἵματος ᾖ, μήπω δ’ ἀκριβῶς ὀξεῖα, κάλλιον ἴσως (μελαγχολικὸς χυμὸς ἕως οὗ λέγεται) μηδὲ καλεῖν μηδέπω τὸν τοιοῦτον χυμὸν μέλαιναν χολὴν, ἀλλ’, ὥσπερ εἴωθα, μελαγχολικὸν, ὅτι τοῦ γενέσθαι μέλαιναν χολὴν πλησίον ἥκει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μελαγχολικὸς χυμὸς ἕως οὗ λέγεται

And the very symptoms of them that have now been mentioned, apart from the bad colour, belong to them when they have occupied the brain and the stomachos 577; for sleeplessness and sleep come about on account of the brain, while appetites and losses of appetite come about on account of the stomachos, which is disposed differently at different times. And it is clear that the mouth of the stomach has here been called stomachos, in which the phlegm that has gathered, as was said a little earlier, produced the appetites; but of the bilious humours, in the case of the yellow they will be thirsty and nauseous, and their mouth is bitter; whereas in the case of the black, they are despondent, sullen, fearful, without thirst, and full of saliva, but not all of them averse to food, only some of them. And those are considerably hungry in whom the black bile has the acid quality predominating; and this comes about when it is rendered more malignant. But so long as it is merely like a sediment of blood, and not yet precisely acid, it is perhaps better (so far as it is called a melancholic humour) not yet to call such a humour black bile either, but, as I am accustomed, melancholic, because it has come near to becoming black bile.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μελαγχολικὸς χυμὸς ἕως οὗ λέγεται, τὰς ὀρέξεις ἐποίησε

And these very symptoms of theirs just now stated, except for the loss of colour, belong to [humours] that have taken possession of the brain and the stomach 577; for sleeplessness and sleep come about on account of the brain, and appetites and want of appetite on account of the stomach, as it is disposed now in one way, now in another. It is clear that by 'stomach' (stomachos) is now meant the mouth of the belly (gastēr), in which the phlegm that has collected, as was said a little before, produced the appetites. Of the bilious, those with yellow bile will be thirsty and nauseated, and their mouth is bitter; those with black bile are despondent, sullen, fearful, free of thirst and full of saliva; not all of them are averse to food, but some of them are. Those in whom the black [bile] has the sour quality predominating are considerably hungry; and this comes about when it is rendered more malignant. But so long as it is only like a sediment of blood, and not yet strictly sour, it is perhaps better (until it is called melancholic humour) not yet to call such a humour black bile at all, but, as I am accustomed, melancholic, because it has come close to becoming black bile.

Greek (Kühn)

ἀλλ’ οὐ νῦν καιρὸς ὑπὲρ τῆς ἐν ταῖς κακοχυμίαις διεξέρχεσθαι διαφορᾶς, ὅτι μηδὲ πλῆθος εἶπον ὀνομάζεσθαι αὐτάς· ἡμῖν δὲ οὐχ ἁπλῶς τὸ πλεονάζον ὁπωσοῦν ἐξευρεῖν, ἀλλὰ περὶ πλήθους ﻿ 578 σκέψασθαι προὔκειτο· πλεονεξία δέ ἐστι τοῦτο τῶν καθ’ ὅλον τὸ σῶμα τοῦ ζώου χυμῶν. οὔτ’ οὖν τῶν κατὰ μέρος ὄγκων οὔτε κακοχυμίας ἀκούειν χρὴ ποθεῖν ἐν τῷ λόγῳ τῷδε τὰς ἰδέας, ἀλλ’ ὅταν ἤτοι κατὰ τὴν ἀρχαίαν ἀναλογίαν αὐξηθῶσιν ἅπαντες, ἤ τινα βραχεῖαν ὑπεροχὴν ἐξ αὐτῶν εἷς λάβῃ. τὸ μὲν οὖν αἷμα παμπόλλην ὑπεροχὴν δύναται λαβεῖν ἐν ταῖς πληθωρικαῖς διαθέσεσι, τῶν δὲ ἄλλων χυμῶν οὐδείς· κακοχυμία γὰρ ἤδη τό γε τοιοῦτον, οὐκ ἔτι πλῆθος, οὐδὲ πληθώρα καλεῖται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But now is not the occasion to go through the difference among the cacochymiai, since I said that they are not even called plethos; for our proposed task was not simply to discover what is in excess in whatever manner, but to inquire about plethos; 578 and this is an excess of the humours throughout the whole body of the animal. One ought not, then, to desire to hear in this discourse about the forms either of the local swellings or of cacochymia, but about when either all the humours are increased according to their original proportion, or one of them acquires some slight preponderance. Now blood can acquire a very great preponderance in the plethoric dispositions, but none of the other humours can; for such a thing is already cacochymia, and is no longer called plethos, nor plethora.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀκούειν χρὴ ποθεῖν

But this is not the time to go through the differences among the cacochymias, since I said that they are not even called plenitude. Our proposed task was not simply to find out whatever is in excess in any way whatsoever, but to examine plenitude 578; and this is an excess of the humours throughout the whole body of the animal. So in this discourse one must not long to hear the kinds either of swellings in particular parts or of cacochymia, but only of the case when either all the humours have increased in their original proportion, or one of them has acquired some slight preponderance. Now blood can acquire a very great preponderance in plethoric conditions, but none of the other humours can; for such a thing is by then called cacochymia, and no longer plenitude or plethora.

Greek (Kühn)

διὸ καὶ σφυγμοὶ κατὰ μὲν τὰς κακοχυμίας οἰκεῖοι τῇ φύσει τῶν χυμῶν ἀκριβῶς εἰσιν· ἐπὶ δὲ τοῖς εἰρημένοις πλήθεσιν οὐχ ὁμοίως ἐναργεῖς ἀποσώζουσι τὰς διαφορὰς, ὅτι σὺν αἵματι δαψιλεῖ πάντως ἐστὶν ὁ πλεονάζων χυμός· ὅταν δὲ κρατήσῃ σφοδρῶς, ἐπὶ μὲν τῇ ξανθῇ χολῇ τοιούτους ἀνάγκη γενέσθαι σφυγμοὺς, οἵους εἴπομεν ἕπεσθαι, ξηραινομένης τε καὶ θερμαινομένη τῆς καρδίας· ἐπὶ δὲ τῇ μελαίνῃ τοιούτους αὖ πάλιν, οἵους ἔφαμεν, εἰ ψύχοιτό τε καὶ ξηραίνοιτο, γίγνεσθαι· κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν 579 λόγον, εἰ νικήσειέν ποτε τὸ φλέγμα τοὺς ἄλλους, ψυχρᾶς καὶ ὑγρᾶς διαθέσεως ἐκγόνους ἀποτελέσει σφυγμούς. αἵματι δὲ τῷ μὲν ἀκριβῶς κατὰ φύσιν οὐδεμία τῆς ἀρχαίας καταστάσεως ἀλλοίωσις ἕπεται· τῷ δὲ ὑγροτέρῳ πλεονάζοντι καὶ οἱ σφυγμοὶ συναλλοιοῦνται, καθάπερ εἰ θερμότερόν τε καὶ ψυχρότερον ἑαυτοῦ γένοιτο. διῄρηται δὲ ἐν τῇ περὶ τῶν σφυγμῶν πραγματείᾳ τὰ τοιαῦτα σύμπαντα· νῦν δὲ ἀρκέσει τό γε τοσοῦτον εἰπεῖν, ὡς ἐξ ἀργίας μὲν καὶ διαίτης ὑγρᾶς ὑγρότερον ἑαυτοῦ γένοιτ’ ἂν τὸ αἷμα, πλεονεξίᾳ τῶν ὑδατωδῶν περιττωμάτων· ἐπὶ θυμῷ δὲ καὶ γυμνασίοις, ἐγκαύσεσί τε καὶ τρίψεσι, καὶ φαρμάκῳ θερμαίνοντι, καὶ τροφῇ τοιαύτῃ, καὶ οἴνοις ἀκρατεστέροις καὶ παλαιοτέροις θερμότερον, ὥσπέρ γε καὶ διά τινα ψύξιν ὑπόγυιον ψυχρότερον. εἰ δὲ ἐπὶ πλέον ἐδέσμασι χρήσαιτό τις ξηροτέροις τὴν κρᾶσιν, οὐ μὴν ἄλλην τινὰ ἔχουσι δυσκρασίαν, ξηρότερον ἐκεῖνο γένοιτ’ ἂν τὸ αἷμα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: θερμαινομένη, ἐγκαύσεσί

Hence also the pulses in the cacochymias are precisely proper to the nature of the humours; but in the cases of plethos that have been mentioned they do not preserve their differences with equal clarity, because the humour that is in excess is in every case accompanied by abundant blood. But when it prevails strongly, in the case of yellow bile the pulses must necessarily become such as we said follow when the heart is being dried and heated; and in the case of black bile, such in turn as we said arise if it is cooled and dried; and by the same 579 reasoning, if phlegm should ever overcome the others, it will produce pulses that are the offspring of a cold and moist disposition. Upon blood that is precisely in accordance with nature no alteration of the original condition follows; but when moister blood is in excess, the pulses too are altered along with it, just as if it should become hotter or colder than itself. All such matters have been distinguished in the treatise on the pulses; for now it will suffice to say only this much, that from idleness and a moist regimen the blood would become moister than itself, through an excess of the watery residues; and from anger and exercises, from exposures to scorching heat and from rubbings, from a heating drug, from nourishment of that kind, and from wines less diluted and older, hotter; just as also through some recent chilling, colder. And if someone should for a longer time use foods that are drier in their mixture, yet have no other dyskrasia, that blood would become drier.

For this reason the pulses too, in cacochymias, are precisely proper to the nature of the humours; but in the plethoras mentioned they do not preserve their differences equally clearly, because the humour that is in excess is in every case together with abundant blood. But when it prevails strongly, with yellow bile the pulses must necessarily become such as we said follow when the heart is dried and heated; with black bile, in turn, such as we stated arise if it should be cooled and dried; and by the same 579 reasoning, if phlegm should ever overcome the others, it will produce pulses that are the offspring of a cold and moist condition. With blood that is exactly in accordance with nature no alteration of the original state follows; but when blood that is moister is in excess, the pulses too are altered along with it, just as if it should become hotter or colder than itself. All such matters have been distinguished in the treatise on the pulses; for now it will suffice to say this much: that from idleness and a moist regimen the blood would become moister than itself, through an excess of the watery residues; hotter, upon anger and exercises, exposures to burning heat and rubbings, a heating drug, food of that kind, and wines less diluted and older; just as, again, colder through some recent chilling. And if someone should make rather extensive use of foods that are drier in their mixture, but that have no other dyscrasia, that blood would become drier.

καὶ δὴ καὶ κατὰ συζυγίαν ὑγρότερόν τε καὶ ψυχρότερον, ἢ ψυχρότερόν τε καὶ ξηρότερον, θερμότερόν τε καὶ ὑγρότερον, ἢ θερμότερόν τε καὶ ξηρότερον ἐπὶ τοῖς εἰρημένοις 580 αἰτίοις ἀποτελεσθήσεται, χωρὶς τοῦ τῶν ἄλλων τινὰ πλεονεκτῆσαι χυμῶν. εἰ δὲ καὶ διὰ τὴν ἐκείνων μίξιν εἰς δυσκρασίαν ἀφίκοιτο, μετὰ κακοχυμίας ἂν ἡ τοιαύτη δυσκρασία συσταίη, λεγομένου μὲν ἔτι καὶ νῦν ἤτοι θερμοτέρου γεγονέναι τοῦ αἵματος, ἢ ψυχροτέρου τε καὶ ὑγροτέρου, καὶ κατὰ τὰς ἑτέρας δυσκρασίας ἓξ, ὀκτὼ γὰρ ἦσαν αἱ σύμπασαι, καθ’ ἕτερον δέ τοι τρόπον, οὐχ ὡς ὁπότε μόνον αὐτὸ καθ’ ἑαυτὸ τοιοῦτον ὑπῆρχεν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And indeed also by way of conjunction it will be rendered moister and colder, or colder and drier, hotter and moister, or hotter and drier, in consequence of the aforementioned 580 causes, without any one of the other humours having gained the upper hand. But if it should also arrive at a dyscrasia through the admixture of those humours, such a dyscrasia would be constituted together with cacochymia, the blood being still, even now, said to have become either hotter, or colder and moister, and likewise in the case of the other six dyscrasias—for there were eight in all—but in a different manner, not as when it was such only in and by itself.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κατὰ συζυγίαν, ἤτοι θερμοτέρου ... ἢ ψυχροτέρου τε καὶ ὑγροτέρου, καθ’ ἕτερον δέ τοι τρόπον

And indeed, in conjugation, the blood will be rendered moister and colder, or colder and drier, hotter and moister, or hotter and drier, as a result of the aforementioned 580 causes, without any of the other humours having come to predominate. But if it should also arrive at a dyscrasia through the mixture of those humours, such a dyscrasia would be constituted together with cacochymia. The blood is still said, even now, to have become either hotter, or colder and moister, and so likewise in the case of the other six dyscrasias, for there were eight in all; but this is meant in a different way, not as when the blood alone, by itself, was of such a kind.

Greek (Kühn)

οἱ σφυγμοὶ δὲ κατὰ τὰς δυσκρασίας τὰς ὀκτὼ τὴν ἰδέαν ἐκείνην ἕξουσιν, οἵαν ἐν τῇ τῶν σφυγμῶν πραγματείᾳ διήλθομεν· εἴτε δ’, ὡς εἵρηται, μόνου τοῦ αἵματός ἐστιν, εἴτε διά τινα ἐπιμιξίαν χυμοῦ, διορίζεσθαι χρὴ τῇ τε συνούσῃ χρόᾳ παντὸς τοῦ σώματος καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις συμπτώμασιν, ὅσα περὶ ὕπνου καὶ ἐγρηγόρσεως, ἢ ὀρέξεως καὶ ἀνορεξίας, ἢ εἴτι τοιοῦτον, μικρὸν ἔμπροσθεν εἴρηται, καὶ τῇ προηγησαμένῃ διαίτῃ, καὶ τοῖς γυμνασίοις, καὶ τοῖς αὐτῷ τῷ κάμνοντι συμπίπτειν εἰθισμένοις, ἔτι τε τῇ ὥρᾳ καὶ τῇ χώρᾳ καὶ τῇ φυσικῇ πυκνότητι καὶ ἀραιότητι τοῦ σώματος· ἄμεινον γὰρ ἐκ πάντων τὸ πιστὸν τῇ διαγνώσει λαμβάνειν, οὐκ ἐξ ἑνὸς ἢ δυοῖν, εὖ εἰδότας ὡς αὐτὸ μόνον ἐν τῇ 581 τέχνῃ πλεῖστα κεκοινώνηκε τοῦ στοχάζεσθαι, λέγω δὴ τὸ μέρος τῆς τέχνης τοῦτο τὸ διαγνωστικὸν ἅπαν, οὐ μόνον τὸ κατὰ τὴν κακοχυμίαν, ἢ τὸ πλῆθος, ἃ νῦν ἡμῖν διορίζεσθαι πρόκειται· τὰ γὰρ περὶ τὰς ἐνεργείας, ἢ τὰς τῶν νοσημάτων αἰτίας, ἢ τὰς ἰάσεις, ἐπιστημονικώτερον ἔχει τὴν γνῶσιν. εἴρηται δ’ ἐπὶ πλέον ὑπὲρ αὐτῶν τούτων ἑτέρωθι, καὶ νῦν ἀρκέσει τὸ προστεθὲν αὐτάρκως διορισαμένους, αὐτόθι που καταπαῦσαι τὸ γράμμα. γίγνοιτο δ’ ἂν αὐτάρκης ὁ λόγος ἐπὶ παραδείγματι ἐξετασθείς. ἔστω δὴ τὸν μὲν ἄνθρωπον ἀπύρεκτον ὑπάρχειν, ἐν ὄγκῳ δὲ μείζονι τὰς φλέβας αὐτῷ καὶ τὸ σύμπαν σῶμα ἐρυθρότερον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

The pulses, in accordance with the eight dyscrasias, will have that form which we went through in the treatise on the pulses; and whether, as has been said, it belongs to the blood alone, or is due to some admixture of a humour, one must distinguish by the colour accompanying the whole body and by the other symptoms—as many as have been mentioned a little earlier concerning sleep and wakefulness, or appetite and lack of appetite, or anything else of the kind—and by the regimen that has preceded, and the exercises, and the things that habitually befall the patient himself, and further by the season and the region and the natural density and rarity of the body; for it is better to take the assurance for the diagnosis from all of these, not from one or two, knowing well that this alone in the 581 art has the greatest share in conjecture—I mean this whole diagnostic part of the art, not only that which concerns cacochymia or plethos, which it is now our task to distinguish; for the matters concerning the activities, or the causes of diseases, or the cures, have a knowledge that is more scientific. These very matters have been discussed at greater length elsewhere, and now it will suffice, having adequately determined what has been added, to bring the treatise to a close at just about this point. And the account would become sufficient if examined on an example. Let it be supposed, then, that the man is free of fever, but that his veins are of greater volume and his whole body redder.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἰδέαν, τὸ προστεθὲν αὐτάρκως διορισαμένους, αὐτόθι που καταπαῦσαι τὸ γράμμα, κεκοινώνηκε τοῦ στοχάζεσθαι

The pulses in the eight dyscrasias will have that form which we set out in the treatise on pulses. Whether, as has been said, it belongs to the blood alone, or arises from some admixture of a humour, one must determine by the accompanying colour of the whole body and by the other symptoms (those concerning sleep and waking, or appetite and lack of appetite, or anything of that kind, which were stated a little earlier), and by the preceding regimen, and the exercises, and the things that are accustomed to befall the patient himself, and further by the season and the country and the natural density and rarity of the body. For it is better to take the assurance for the diagnosis from all of these, not from one or two, knowing well that this alone in the 581 art has the largest share in conjecture: I mean this whole diagnostic part of the art, not only that which concerns cacochymia or plethora, which we now propose to distinguish. For matters concerning the functions, or the causes of diseases, or the cures, admit of a more scientific knowledge. More has been said about these very matters elsewhere, and for now it will suffice, once we have adequately determined what has been added, to bring the writing to a close somewhere about here. The account would become adequate if it were examined by way of an example. Let it be, then, that the man is without fever, but that his veins are of greater bulk and his whole body redder.

Greek (Kühn)

σκεπτέον οὖν, εἰ μηδὲν τούτῳ τῶν θερμαινόντων αἰτίων ἐπλησίασεν· εἰ γάρ τοι φαίνοιτο, βέλτιον ἀναβάλλεσθαι τὴν διάγνωσιν, ἄχρι περ ἂν ἡ ἐξ ἐκείνου κίνησις ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ σώματι καταστῇ, διότι μήτε θυμούμενον, ἢ λελουμένον, ἢ λουόμενον, ἢ γεγυμνασμένον, ἢ οἴνου προσενηνεγμένον ἀρτίως, ἢ ἄλλως ὁπωσοῦν τεθερμασμένον εἰς τὴν διάγνωσιν ἄγειν προσήκει, ἀλλ’ ἐν ἡσυχίᾳ πασῶν τῶν ἔξωθεν ὑπάρχοντα κινήσεων· εἰ γὰρ ἐν ἐκείνῳ 582 τῷ καιρῷ φαίνοιτο τοιοῦτος, αἷμα πλεονάζει τῷ ἀνθρώπῳ. τοὐντεῦθεν ἐπισκέπτεσθαι τἄλλα, πρῶτα μὲν ὅσα μεγίστην ἔχει δύναμιν, εἶτα ἑξῆς τὰ λοιπά· ἐδείχθη δὲ βάρος καὶ τάσις, ὅ τε πρὸς τὰς κινήσεις ὄκνος, εἶθ’ ἑξῆς τὰ κατὰ τοὺς σφυγμοὺς, ἀγρυπνίας τε καὶ ὕπνους, ὀρέξεις τε καὶ ἀνορεξίας, ὅσα τ’ ἄλλα τοιαῦτα. μετὰ δὲ ταῦτα τῶν προηγησαμένων ἕκαστον, ὅσα πλῆθος ἐργάζεται, μή τις συνήθης ἔκκρισις ἐπέσχηται, μὴ γυμνάσιον ἠμέληται, μὴ πλείω καὶ πολύτροφα σιτία συνεχῶς προσενήνεκται, μὴ μετὰ τροφὴν ἀήθεσι χρῆται λουτροῖς, καὶ πρὸς τούτοις ἔτι μὴ πολλάκις ὁ τοιοῦτος ἄνθρωπος ἁλίσκεται νοσήμασι πληθωρικοῖς.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

One must therefore consider whether none of the heating causes has come near this man; for if it should appear that one has, it is better to postpone the diagnosis until the movement arising from that cause has settled down throughout the whole body, since it is not fitting to bring to diagnosis a man who is angry, or has bathed, or is bathing, or has exercised, or has just taken wine, or has been heated in any other way whatsoever, but one who is at rest from all external movements; for if at that 582 time he should appear of this sort, blood is in excess in the man. From there one should examine the other signs, first those that have the greatest force, then the rest in order; it was shown that heaviness and tension have this, and the reluctance towards movements, then next the matters pertaining to the pulses, wakefulness and sleep, appetites and lack of appetite, and whatever other things are of this kind. After these, each of the antecedent causes that produce plethos: whether some customary excretion has been held back, whether exercise has been neglected, whether foods in greater quantity and highly nourishing have been taken continually, whether he makes use of unaccustomed baths after food, and in addition to these, further, whether such a man is frequently seized by plethoric diseases.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: λουόμενον, κίνησις ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ σώματι καταστῇ

One must therefore consider whether any of the heating causes has come near him; for if one should appear to have done so, it is better to postpone the diagnosis until the motion arising from it has settled down in the whole body, since it is not proper to bring to diagnosis someone who is angry, or has bathed, or is bathing, or has exercised, or has just taken wine, or has been heated in any other way whatsoever, but rather one who is at rest from all external motions. For if at that 582 time he should appear such, blood is in excess in the person. From there one should examine the other signs, first those that have the greatest force, then the rest in order. It was shown that these are heaviness and tension, and reluctance towards movements, and then next the features of the pulses, sleeplessness and sleep, appetites and lack of appetite, and whatever other things are of this kind. After these, each of the antecedent factors that produce plethora: whether some habitual excretion has been suppressed, whether exercise has been neglected, whether larger quantities of highly nourishing foods have been taken continually, whether he makes use of unaccustomed baths after food, and in addition to these, whether such a person is frequently seized by plethoric diseases.

K7.582-583K. VII 582–583, ch. 11 difficulty high (4.55/100; thin 3, absent 4) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 14/14 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπὶ τούτοις ἅπασι καὶ τὰ παρὰ τῆς χώρας, καὶ ὥρας, ἢ εἴτι τοιοῦτον ἄλλο προσθεὶς, οὐκ ἴσην ἅπασι νέμων τὴν αἰτίαν, ὥσπερ οὐδ’ ἐν τοῖς ἄλλοις μέρεσι τῆς τέχνης· ἔνια μὲν γὰρ τῶν γνωρισμάτων ἐστὶν ἐπιστημονικὰ, καθάπερ ἡ τάσις, καὶ τὸ βάρος, ὅ τε ὄγκος τῶν φλεβῶν, ὅ τε ὄκνος, καὶ τὸ χρῶμα, καὶ ἡ τῶν σφυγμῶν κίνησις, ἁπασῶν δηλονότι τῶν προσφάτων κινήσεων ἀφωρισμένων· ἔνια δὲ ἀπολείπεται τούτων, οἷον ἡ τῶν, ἐκκρίσεων 583 ἐπίσχεσις, ὅ τε ἀργὸς βίος, ἐδέσματά τε πλείω καὶ τρόφιμα, καὶ μετὰ τροφὴν λουτρά· συνιόντα μέντοι τὰ σύμπαντα τοῖς ἐπιστημονικοῖς ἐγγὺς ἥκει. τρίτην ἐπὶ τούτοις ἔχει τάξιν, εἰ συνήθως ἁλίσκεται πληθωρικοῖς νοσήμασιν ὁ ἄνθρωπος· ἐφεξῆς δὲ τὰ κατὰ χώρας καὶ ὥρας συστατικὰ πάντων αὐτῶν· ἐν μὲν γὰρ χειμῶνι καὶ ἦρι ψυχραῖς τε καὶ εὐκράτοις χώραις μᾶλλον ἀθροίζουσι τὸ πλῆθος· ἐν δὲ ταῖς θερμαῖς καὶ ξηραῖς ὥραις καὶ χώραις ἧττον· οὕτως δὲ καὶ οἱ μὲν πυκνοὶ τὴν ἐπιφάνειαν μᾶλλον, οἱ δ’ ἀραιοὶ σπανιώτερον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συστατικὰ

In addition to all these, adding also the factors deriving from the region and the season, or anything else of this kind, not assigning an equal share of causation to all, just as one does not in the other parts of the art either; for some of the signs are scientific, such as tension, heaviness, the swelling of the veins, reluctance, the colour, and the movement of the pulses—all recent movements, of course, having been set aside; but some fall short of these, such as the suppression of the excretions, 583 an idle life, foods more abundant and more nourishing, and baths after food; yet when all of these come together they come close to the scientific ones. Third in rank after these is whether the person is habitually seized by plethoric diseases; and next the factors relating to regions and seasons, which are constitutive of all of them; for in winter and spring, and in cold and temperate regions, people accumulate plethora more, but in hot and dry seasons and regions less; and likewise those who are dense in their surface do so more, while those who are loose-textured do so more rarely.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προσθεὶς (nominative participle with no finite verb; the construction is elliptical), νέμων τὴν αἰτίαν (taken as 'assigning importance/weight'), συστατικὰ πάντων αὐτῶν (sense of συστατικά: 'contributing to the formation of'), ἡ τῶν, ἐκκρίσεων (stray comma in Kühn)

On top of all these, one adds also the indications from the region and the season, or anything else of this kind, without assigning equal importance to all of them, just as one does not in the other parts of the art either. For some of the signs are scientific, such as the tension, the heaviness, the swelling of the veins, the reluctance [to move], the colour, and the movement of the pulses, provided, obviously, that all recent motions have been excluded. Others fall short of these, such as the retention of the excretions, 583 the idle life, foods that are more abundant and nourishing, and baths after food; yet all of them together come close to the scientific ones. Third in rank after these is whether the person is habitually seized by plethoric diseases. Next come the things belonging to regions and seasons that contribute to the formation of all of them. For in winter and spring, and in cold and temperate regions, people accumulate plethora more; in hot and dry seasons and regions, less. So too those who are dense at the surface accumulate it more, and those who are rarefied more rarely.
